Evaluating the ethical implications of these models is crucial for patient safety and responsible AI development. PrinciplismQA fills a gap in current benchmarks, providing a tool for identifying and mitigating potential ethical risks. The findings regarding the knowledge-action gap and the struggle with beneficence are particularly relevant and highlight areas for future research.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive and well-structured benchmark:** The benchmark covers a wide range of scenarios and ethical principles.
    *   **Expert validation:**  The validation process ensures the accuracy and relevance of the questions and answer keys.
    *   **Systematic evaluation protocol:**  The LLM-as-a-Judge framework offers a scalable method for evaluating open-ended responses.
    *   **Clear and actionable findings:**  The results identify specific ethical weaknesses in current LLMs.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited exploration of alternative ethical frameworks:** While Principlism is a widely recognized framework, exploring other ethical theories (e.g., virtue ethics, deontology) could provide a more comprehensive assessment.
    *   **Potential biases in case studies:** The case studies are sourced from a single journal (AMA Journal of Ethics), which may introduce biases in the scenarios and expert commentaries.
    *   **Dependency on GPT-4 for evaluation:** The LLM-as-a-Judge approach relies on GPT-4 for scoring open-ended responses, which can introduce its own biases and limitations.  Although the authors are using GPT-4 to evaluate LLMs, this dependency reduces the reproducibility of this method.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The benchmark will likely be adopted by researchers and developers working on medical LLMs.
    *   The findings will inform the development of more ethically aligned models.
    *   The paper could stimulate further research on ethical evaluation methodologies for AI in healthcare.

**Rigorous Rationale:**

The creation of PrinciplismQA is a significant contribution, offering a more structured and in-depth method of examining the ethical complexities of LLMs in healthcare, moving beyond broad assertions about fairness or safety. However, the paper could have strengthened its analysis by investigating diverse ethical frameworks in addition to Principlism, providing a more holistic understanding of ethical considerations. While reliance on GPT-4 presents a practical solution for automated evaluation, the authors do not fully discuss the implications or methods to measure and mitigate the potential biases involved, affecting reproducibility. The single-source limitation of the case studies adds another layer to the bias issue. Even considering the above limitations, the thoroughness of the study and the clear identification of the "knowledge-action gap" in particular, hold considerable promise.

**Score: 7.5**
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Speech LLMs in Low-Resource Scenarios: Data Volume Requirements and the Impact of Pretraining on High-Resource Languages
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05149v1)
- **Authors**: Seraphina Fong, Marco Matassoni, Alessio Brutti
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated potential in handling spoken inputs for high-resource languages, reaching state-of-the-art performance in various tasks. However, their applicability is still less explored in low-resource settings. This work investigates the use of Speech LLMs for low-resource Automatic Speech Recognition using the SLAM-ASR framework, where a trainable lightweight projector connects a speech encoder and a LLM. Firstly, we assess training data volume requirements to match Whisper-only performance, re-emphasizing the challenges of limited data. Secondly, we show that leveraging mono- or multilingual projectors pretrained on high-resource languages reduces the impact of data scarcity, especially with small training sets. Using multilingual LLMs (EuroLLM, Salamandra) with whisper-large-v3-turbo, we evaluate performance on several public benchmarks, providing insights for future research on optimizing Speech LLMs for low-resource languages and multilinguality.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, including a novelty and significance score.

**Summary:**

The paper "Speech LLMs in Low-Resource Scenarios: Data Volume Requirements and the Impact of Pretraining on High-Resource Languages" investigates the applicability of Speech Large Language Models (LLMs) for Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) in low-resource languages. It focuses on the SLAM-ASR framework, which uses a trainable linear projector to connect a frozen speech encoder (Whisper-large-v3-turbo) with a frozen LLM. The authors examine the impact of training data volume on the projector's performance, and the benefits of pretraining the projector on high-resource languages (English and Spanish) before fine-tuning it on low-resource languages (Italian and Galician). The paper explores different multilingual LLMs (EuroLLM 1.7B, Salamandra 2B), and how they perform with limited data. Key findings include the need for a significant amount of training data (around 200 hours) to achieve competitive performance with Whisper-only models, and the value of pretraining the projector on high-resource data, which can improve performance, especially with small fine-tuning datasets.  Finally, the use of a multilingual projector bootstrapped with a combination of various languages performs the best.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Relevant Problem:**  Addressing low-resource speech recognition is critically important as it directly impacts the inclusivity and accessibility of speech technology.
    *   **Systematic Exploration:** The paper performs a methodical analysis, systematically varying training data volumes and pretraining strategies. The research questions are well-defined, and the experimental setup is relatively thorough.
    *   **Clear Results and Analysis:**  The results are clearly presented in tables, and the authors provide a reasonably detailed discussion of their findings. They also consider the domain mismatch issue, which is a common challenge.
    *   **Utilizing an Established Framework:** By using the SLAM-ASR framework, the work builds upon an existing, validated system, which increases the reliability of the results. It provides a controlled environment to experiment with low-resource languages.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Novelty:**  The use of pretraining and transfer learning in low-resource settings is not entirely new. The paper applies existing techniques to the Speech LLM context. The choice of SLAM-ASR, while providing reproducibility, limits potential exploration of architectural innovations beyond what is dictated by the framework.
    *   **Incremental Contribution:**  While the analysis is useful, the findings are somewhat incremental.  The conclusion that more data helps, or that pretraining improves performance, is largely expected. The specific quantification of "how much" is helpful, but not transformative.
    *   **Limited Scope of LLMs:** The choice of EuroLLM and Salamandra is reasonable, but the investigation could benefit from a broader range of LLMs (even if computationally expensive), especially considering the rapid evolution of LLM technology. The work would have been more significant if it had explored the behavior of more recent, more powerful LLMs in the low-resource speech domain.
    *   **Performance Ceiling by Whisper-Only Baseline:** The paper uses Whisper-only model's performance as target ASR performance. Even though SLAM-ASR achieved this goal with 200 hours of data with some data and LLM combinations, this is not particularly exciting.  It primarily shows that SLAM-ASR, which is a framework built for English, can catch up Whisper on other languages.
    *   **Narrow Language Focus:** The study primarily focuses on European languages. While valuable, extending the investigation to languages from different language families could increase the generality of the findings.
    *   **Limited exploration of parameters:** The method section briefly mentioned how a small amount of LoRA was helpful. Further explorations of the many parameter choices (such as different prompt techniques) could add more to the impact of the paper.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper provides empirical evidence on the data requirements and pretraining benefits for speech LLMs in low-resource ASR. This has practical implications for researchers and practitioners working on building ASR systems for under-represented languages.
    *   It highlights the importance of carefully selecting and adapting pretraining strategies for low-resource speech recognition.
    *   It identifies areas for future research, such as exploring other LLMs, and experimenting with multilingual training and fine-tuning techniques.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper addresses a relevant problem with a systematic approach and clear results. However, the novelty is limited, and the contribution is somewhat incremental. The paper could benefit from a broader investigation of LLMs and languages, and more focus on parameter choices that could improve the SLAM-ASR framework in low-resource settings. Therefore, I assign a score that reflects these strengths and weaknesses.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: eess.AS
- **Score**: 6/10

### Tool Graph Retriever: Exploring Dependency Graph-based Tool Retrieval for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05152v1)
- **Authors**: Linfeng Gao, Yaoxiang Wang, Minlong Peng, Jialong Tang, Yuzhe Shang, Mingming Sun, Jinsong Su
- **Abstract**: With the remarkable advancement of AI agents, the number of their equipped tools is increasing rapidly. However, integrating all tool information into the limited model context becomes impractical, highlighting the need for efficient tool retrieval methods. In this regard, dominant methods primarily rely on semantic similarities between tool descriptions and user queries to retrieve relevant tools. However, they often consider each tool independently, overlooking dependencies between tools, which may lead to the omission of prerequisite tools for successful task execution. To deal with this defect, in this paper, we propose Tool Graph Retriever (TGR), which exploits the dependencies among tools to learn better tool representations for retrieval. First, we construct a dataset termed TDI300K to train a discriminator for identifying tool dependencies. Then, we represent all candidate tools as a tool dependency graph and use graph convolution to integrate the dependencies into their representations. Finally, these updated tool representations are employed for online retrieval. Experimental results on several commonly used datasets show that our TGR can bring a performance improvement to existing dominant methods, achieving SOTA performance. Moreover, in-depth analyses also verify the importance of tool dependencies and the effectiveness of our TGR.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Tool Graph Retriever (TGR), a novel approach to tool retrieval for large language model (LLM) agents.  TGR addresses the limitation of existing methods that primarily focus on semantic similarity between user queries and tool descriptions, often overlooking dependencies between tools.  TGR constructs a tool dependency graph, using a discriminator trained on a newly created dataset (TDI300K) to identify dependencies. Graph convolution is then applied to integrate these dependencies into tool representations, enhancing retrieval accuracy.  Experiments on API-Bank and ToolBench datasets demonstrate that TGR improves performance, achieving state-of-the-art results, and highlighting the significance of tool dependencies in retrieval.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its explicit modeling of tool dependencies for tool retrieval within the context of LLM-based agents. While graph convolutional networks (GCNs) and semantic similarity-based retrieval have been individually explored, the combination of these techniques to incorporate tool dependencies is a novel contribution. The creation of the TDI300K dataset also represents a valuable contribution that could facilitate further research in this area. This addresses the major problem of many independent tools causing less tools to work together.

*   **Significance:** Addressing the limitation of existing tool retrieval methods that disregard dependencies is significant for several reasons. First, it leads to more robust and effective tool usage by LLM agents, reducing errors caused by missing prerequisite tools. This in turn boosts the agent's overall task completion rate and improves user experience. Second, by introducing a structured approach to representing tool dependencies, the paper opens up avenues for more sophisticated tool orchestration and planning strategies. This is very important in the current industry as there are many agents working together to solve a task.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined problem:** The paper clearly identifies a practical limitation in current tool retrieval methods.
    *   **Novel approach:** TGR offers a unique way to address the identified problem by incorporating tool dependencies.
    *   **Comprehensive experiments:** The experimental results on API-Bank and ToolBench provide strong evidence for the effectiveness of TGR.
    *   **Creation of TDI300K dataset:**  The release of this dataset is a valuable contribution that fosters further research.
    *   **In-depth analysis:** The paper includes insightful analyses of the impact of different dependency graph construction methods and graph densities.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Discriminator Accuracy:** The effectiveness of TGR is heavily reliant on the accuracy of the tool dependency discriminator. While the discriminator achieves decent performance, improvements in its accuracy could further enhance the benefits of TGR. The discriminator's accuracy can be further improved.
    *   **Time Complexity:** The O(N^2) complexity of graph construction, although mentioned, could be a practical limitation for systems with a very large number of tools.
    *   **Limited Scope of Dependencies:** The paper focuses on specific types of dependencies (input/output and verification). Other dependency types might be relevant and could be incorporated. This is not explained in the paper.
    *   **Limited Evaluation of Generalization:** The evaluations primarily focus on existing datasets. It is very important to evaluate on new dataset and tools.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more intelligent and reliable tool-augmented LLM agents. By explicitly modeling tool dependencies, it enables more sophisticated tool orchestration and planning, paving the way for more complex and automated task execution.

**Overall:** The paper presents a solid contribution with a clearly identified problem, a novel solution, and strong experimental results. While there are some limitations related to discriminator accuracy, graph construction complexity, and the scope of dependency types considered, the core idea of incorporating tool dependencies is significant and impactful.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a significant advance over current tool retrieval strategies by integrating tool dependencies. The creation of the TDI300K dataset and the extensive experimental results on benchmark datasets bolster the importance of the contribution. The work is very important as many tools are now working together to solve a task in complex AI agents. While limitations like discriminator accuracy and computational complexity exist, these areas can be improved in further studies. The novel method and useful dataset make this paper significant.

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### PhysPatch: A Physically Realizable and Transferable Adversarial Patch Attack for Multimodal Large Language Models-based Autonomous Driving Systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05167v1)
- **Authors**: Qi Guo, Xiaojun Jia, Shanmin Pang, Simeng Qin, Lin Wang, Ju Jia, Yang Liu, Qing Guo
- **Abstract**: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) are becoming integral to autonomous driving (AD) systems due to their strong vision-language reasoning capabilities. However, MLLMs are vulnerable to adversarial attacks, particularly adversarial patch attacks, which can pose serious threats in real-world scenarios. Existing patch-based attack methods are primarily designed for object detection models and perform poorly when transferred to MLLM-based systems due to the latter's complex architectures and reasoning abilities. To address these limitations, we propose PhysPatch, a physically realizable and transferable adversarial patch framework tailored for MLLM-based AD systems. PhysPatch jointly optimizes patch location, shape, and content to enhance attack effectiveness and real-world applicability. It introduces a semantic-based mask initialization strategy for realistic placement, an SVD-based local alignment loss with patch-guided crop-resize to improve transferability, and a potential field-based mask refinement method. Extensive experiments across open-source, commercial, and reasoning-capable MLLMs demonstrate that PhysPatch significantly outperforms prior methods in steering MLLM-based AD systems toward target-aligned perception and planning outputs. Moreover, PhysPatch consistently places adversarial patches in physically feasible regions of AD scenes, ensuring strong real-world applicability and deployability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the PhysPatch paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the vulnerability of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) in autonomous driving (AD) systems to adversarial patch attacks. It introduces PhysPatch, a novel framework that generates physically realizable and transferable adversarial patches specifically designed to mislead MLLM-based AD systems.  PhysPatch jointly optimizes patch location, shape, and content, employing a semantic-based mask initialization strategy, an SVD-based local alignment loss with patch-guided crop-resize, and a potential field-based mask refinement method.  The authors demonstrate that PhysPatch significantly outperforms existing adversarial patch methods in terms of attack success rate, semantic alignment, and visual quality across various open-source, commercial, and reasoning-capable MLLMs.  Importantly, it ensures physical realizability by placing patches in feasible regions within AD scenes.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces several novel components that contribute to its overall novelty:

    *   **Targeted Attack on MLLMs in AD:** Prior work on adversarial attacks was less focused on MLLMs used in autonomous driving and even less focused on physical attacks.
    *   **Semantic-Aware Mask Initialization:** The use of MLLM reasoning to guide the placement of patches in semantically meaningful and physically plausible locations is a significant advancement over random or naive placement strategies.
    *   **SVD-Based Local Alignment Loss with Patch-Guided Crop-Resize:** The SVD-based loss, combined with the tailored cropping strategy, provides a theoretically sound and practically effective way to improve transferability across different models, a crucial aspect for black-box attack scenarios.
    *   **Potential Field-Based Mask Refinement:** Adaptively refining the patch shape within physically feasible regions enhances attack capabilities and real-world realism.
*   **Significance:** The paper has significant implications for the field of autonomous driving safety and security:

    *   **Highlighting Vulnerabilities:**  It demonstrates a practical and potent attack vector against MLLM-based AD systems, exposing a critical vulnerability that could have severe real-world consequences.
    *   **Advancing Adversarial Attack Techniques:** It provides a more sophisticated approach to generating physically realizable adversarial patches, pushing the boundaries of what's possible in attacking complex vision-language systems.
    *   **Informing Robustness Research:**  The findings underscore the need for more robust defenses against adversarial attacks in AD systems and can guide future research in this area.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper presents extensive experimental results across a diverse set of MLLMs, defense mechanisms, and evaluation metrics, demonstrating the effectiveness and robustness of PhysPatch.
    *   **Theoretical Justification:** The paper provides theoretical justification for its key components, such as the SVD-based loss, which strengthens its credibility.
    *   **Practical Realizability:** The focus on physical realizability is crucial for translating research into real-world security considerations.
    *   **Clear and Well-Written:** The paper is well-organized and clearly explains the proposed method and its advantages.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Real-World Testing:** While the paper demonstrates physical realizability by printing and photographing patches, a more rigorous evaluation involving actual autonomous vehicles in real-world scenarios would further strengthen the findings.
    *   **Specific Scenario Dependence**: As mentioned in the paper, the method currently works better in scenarios in which the regions are present.
    *   **Ethical Considerations**: Although the paper highlights potential misuses and ethical issues of adversarial attacks, a more extensive discussion of the ethical implications and mitigation strategies would have been very useful.

**Overall Score and Justification:**

The PhysPatch paper represents a significant contribution to the field of autonomous driving security. It's a well-executed study that identifies a critical vulnerability in MLLM-based AD systems and proposes a novel and effective attack framework. The strengths of the paper in terms of novelty, evaluation, and theoretical justification outweigh its limitations. However, the lack of real autonomous driving evaluations and a potentially under-developed ethical considerations section do need to be acknowledged.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Beyond Pixels: Medical Image Quality Assessment with Implicit Neural Representations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05168v1)
- **Authors**: Caner Özer, Patryk Rygiel, Bram de Wilde, İlkay Öksüz, Jelmer M. Wolterink
- **Abstract**: Artifacts pose a significant challenge in medical imaging, impacting diagnostic accuracy and downstream analysis. While image-based approaches for detecting artifacts can be effective, they often rely on preprocessing methods that can lead to information loss and high-memory-demand medical images, thereby limiting the scalability of classification models. In this work, we propose the use of implicit neural representations (INRs) for image quality assessment. INRs provide a compact and continuous representation of medical images, naturally handling variations in resolution and image size while reducing memory overhead. We develop deep weight space networks, graph neural networks, and relational attention transformers that operate on INRs to achieve image quality assessment. Our method is evaluated on the ACDC dataset with synthetically generated artifact patterns, demonstrating its effectiveness in assessing image quality while achieving similar performance with fewer parameters.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a method for medical image quality assessment (specifically, artifact detection in cardiac MRI) using implicit neural representations (INRs).  The core idea is to encode MRI images as INRs, which are compact and continuous representations, and then train classifiers on the INR parameters (weights) to predict image quality. The paper compares various architectures for classifying INR parameters, including MLPs, deep weight space networks (DWSNets), graph neural networks (GNNs), and relational attention transformers.  The method is evaluated on the ACDC dataset with synthetically generated mistriggering artifacts. The results demonstrate that the INR-based approach achieves comparable or better performance than a pixel-based MLP classifier with significantly fewer parameters. The authors emphasize the reduced memory overhead and potential for scalability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The application of INRs to medical image *quality assessment*, specifically artifact detection, is relatively novel. While INRs have been used for other medical imaging tasks (segmentation, registration), the focus on *quality* from the *INR representation itself* distinguishes this work. Directly classifying image quality from the learned parameters (weights) of an INR, rather than reconstructing the image and then assessing quality, is a key innovative aspect. The comparison of different architectures (MLP, DWSNet, GNN, Transformer) operating on the INR parameters is also a useful contribution, shedding light on how to best leverage this representation for quality assessment. This allows the authors to explore the structural properties of the INR parameters themselves.
*   **Significance:** Medical image artifacts are a pervasive problem, and accurate artifact detection is crucial for reliable diagnosis and downstream analysis. The potential of INRs to reduce memory footprint while preserving essential information could be significant for large-scale datasets and resource-constrained environments. The performance of the INR-based methods, surpassing the pixel-based approach, suggests a promising direction for future research.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-defined methodology with detailed descriptions of the INR construction and classification pipelines.
    *   Thorough experimental evaluation with multiple classifier architectures and comparison against a relevant baseline.
    *   The use of synthetic artifacts, while a limitation (discussed below), allows for controlled experimentation and quantifiable evaluation of artifact detection performance.
    *   Emphasis on parameter efficiency and potential for scalability.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The use of *synthetic* mistriggering artifacts is a major limitation.  Real-world artifacts are often more complex and diverse than the simulated ones.  The performance of the method on real artifact data may be significantly different.
    *   The computational cost of INR optimization is acknowledged, but the paper doesn't offer substantial solutions beyond parallelization. The training time of INRs and the associated computational resources might limit the practicality of the approach.
    *   While the authors claim the method handles variations in resolution, the experiments are performed on fixed-size crops (256x256). Its robustness to different input resolutions has not been thoroughly demonstrated.
    *   The improvement over the baseline, while present, is not dramatically large, and requires more sophisticated models.
    *   The statistical significance analysis of the Transformer's performance compared to other models, indicated by a p-value < 0.01, is positive but needs more elaboration. It is vital to ensure that adequate statistical power has been reached to accurately support the assertion that the Transformer (Probe=32) outperforms other models. The lack of information about the sample size and the statistical test employed weakens this component of the evaluation.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the field of medical image quality assessment by introducing a novel and parameter-efficient approach. However, its true impact depends on its ability to generalize to real-world artifact scenarios and its practical applicability in clinical settings. Future research needs to address the limitations of synthetic data and explore methods for accelerating INR optimization.

**Justification for the Score:**

Considering the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, a score of **7** is justified.

*   The method is novel in its application of INRs to image quality assessment *directly using INR parameters*.
*   The results are promising, showing comparable or better performance than a pixel-based approach with fewer parameters, *demonstrating the ability to use weight space*
*   However, the reliance on synthetic artifacts significantly limits the generalizability and clinical relevance of the findings, a crucial point in medical imaging applications where the variability of artifacts is very high.
*   While the parameter efficiency is attractive, the computational cost of INR optimization needs further address.
*   The statistical analysis should be more extensive.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: eess.IV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Posterior-GRPO: Rewarding Reasoning Processes in Code Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05170v1)
- **Authors**: Lishui Fan, Yu Zhang, Mouxiang Chen, Zhongxin Liu
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement learning (RL) has significantly advanced code generation for large language models (LLMs). However, current paradigms rely on outcome-based rewards from test cases, neglecting the quality of the intermediate reasoning process. While supervising the reasoning process directly is a promising direction, it is highly susceptible to reward hacking, where the policy model learns to exploit the reasoning reward signal without improving final outcomes. To address this, we introduce a unified framework that can effectively incorporate the quality of the reasoning process during RL. First, to enable reasoning evaluation, we develop LCB-RB, a benchmark comprising preference pairs of superior and inferior reasoning processes. Second, to accurately score reasoning quality, we introduce an Optimized-Degraded based (OD-based) method for reward model training. This method generates high-quality preference pairs by systematically optimizing and degrading initial reasoning paths along curated dimensions of reasoning quality, such as factual accuracy, logical rigor, and coherence. A 7B parameter reward model with this method achieves state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance on LCB-RB and generalizes well to other benchmarks. Finally, we introduce Posterior-GRPO (P-GRPO), a novel RL method that conditions process-based rewards on task success. By selectively applying rewards to the reasoning processes of only successful outcomes, P-GRPO effectively mitigates reward hacking and aligns the model's internal reasoning with final code correctness. A 7B parameter model with P-GRPO achieves superior performance across diverse code generation tasks, outperforming outcome-only baselines by 4.5%, achieving comparable performance to GPT-4-Turbo. We further demonstrate the generalizability of our approach by extending it to mathematical tasks. Our models, dataset, and code are publicly available.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Posterior-GRPO: Rewarding Reasoning Processes in Code Generation" addresses the limitations of current reinforcement learning (RL) methods for code generation, which primarily focus on outcome-based rewards (e.g., test case pass rates) and neglect the quality of the intermediate reasoning process.  The authors propose a novel framework consisting of three main components: 1) LCB-RB, a benchmark for evaluating reasoning process quality; 2) an Optimized-Degraded (OD)-based method for training reward models that can accurately score reasoning quality; and 3) Posterior-GRPO (P-GRPO), an RL algorithm that conditions process-based rewards on task success to mitigate reward hacking. P-GRPO only rewards reasoning processes when the final code is correct, aligning internal reasoning with functional correctness. Experiments demonstrate that P-GRPO improves code generation performance across various benchmarks, surpassing outcome-only baselines and achieving results comparable to GPT-4-Turbo. The method's generalizability is further shown through extensions to mathematical tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a novel and well-integrated framework. The key novelty lies in the synergistic combination of three elements: a purpose-built benchmark for evaluating reasoning, a contrastive reward model training method (OD-based), and a modified RL algorithm (P-GRPO) that smartly incorporates reasoning rewards. Individually, some components might have elements present in prior works (like contrastive learning or GRPO), but the specific combination and adaptation to code generation, along with the posterior reward assignment, is novel. The LCB-RB benchmark helps address a crucial gap in code generation research, focusing on the quality of reasoning steps rather than just the end result.

*   **Significance:** Addressing reward hacking in RL for code generation is a significant issue. The paper offers a practical and effective approach, demonstrating improved performance across several benchmarks. The results are impressive and show substantial gains compared to existing outcome-based methods. Achieving comparable performance to GPT-4-Turbo with a 7B model is noteworthy. Furthermore, the generalizability to mathematical tasks strengthens the claim that P-GRPO effectively improves reasoning rather than just overfitting to coding-specific patterns. The public availability of code, models, and datasets will significantly benefit the research community.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined Problem and Solution:** Clearly articulates the limitations of existing approaches and presents a structured solution.
    *   **Rigorous Evaluation:** Comprehensive evaluation across multiple code generation and mathematical benchmarks, including comparisons to strong baselines.
    *   **Addressing Reward Hacking:** Successfully mitigates reward hacking through posterior reward assignment, a key challenge in RL for LLMs.
    *   **Generalizability:** Demonstrates generalizability across code generation and mathematical reasoning tasks.
    *   **Resource Availability:** Publicly releasing code, models, and datasets promotes reproducibility and further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependence on GPT-4 for Filtering:** While using GPT-4 to filter and create preference pairs is understandable, it introduces a dependence on a proprietary model and a potential source of bias. A fully open-source pipeline for benchmark creation would further strengthen the work.
    *   **Computational Cost:** The contrastive reward model training method requires generating optimized and degraded reasoning processes, which can be computationally expensive.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Ablations:** While performance with different reward model integrations are present, a wider variety of ablation studies on P-GRPO components (e.g. the impact of format reward) would strengthen the findings.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field by shifting the focus from outcome-based RL to reasoning-aware RL in code generation. The LCB-RB benchmark provides a valuable resource for evaluating future methods, and P-GRPO offers a practical algorithm for improving reasoning capabilities of LLMs. The modular framework facilitates the integration of other improvements to RL or Reward model training within code generation tasks.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:** The paper makes a significant contribution to the field of RL for code generation. The proposed framework effectively addresses reward hacking and improves reasoning abilities, resulting in substantial performance gains. The novelty is solid, consisting of a well-justified combination of several methods specifically adapted to the code generation context. The limitations of the reliance on GPT-4o for dataset creation and the computational cost are relatively minor, and the overall impact of the work is likely to be high. A higher score (9 or 10) would require a more fundamental breakthrough or a solution that is entirely independent of proprietary models. Nevertheless, the paper is a strong and valuable contribution.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 8/10

### ATLANTIS at SemEval-2025 Task 3: Detecting Hallucinated Text Spans in Question Answering
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05179v1)
- **Authors**: Catherine Kobus, François Lancelot, Marion-Cécile Martin, Nawal Ould Amer
- **Abstract**: This paper presents the contributions of the ATLANTIS team to SemEval-2025 Task 3, focusing on detecting hallucinated text spans in question answering systems. Large Language Models (LLMs) have significantly advanced Natural Language Generation (NLG) but remain susceptible to hallucinations, generating incorrect or misleading content. To address this, we explored methods both with and without external context, utilizing few-shot prompting with a LLM, token-level classification or LLM fine-tuned on synthetic data. Notably, our approaches achieved top rankings in Spanish and competitive placements in English and German. This work highlights the importance of integrating relevant context to mitigate hallucinations and demonstrate the potential of fine-tuned models and prompt engineering.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper describes the ATLANTIS team's approach to SemEval-2025 Task 3, which focuses on detecting hallucinated text spans in question answering (QA) systems. The team explored methods both with and without external context. Methods without context involved few-shot prompting with a Large Language Model (LLM) and fine-tuning a token-level classifier on synthetically generated data.  Methods incorporating external context (retrieved using a RAG system with Wikipedia) involved few-shot prompting, fine-tuning a token-level classifier, and fine-tuning a LLM for hallucination detection. The paper reports results across multiple languages (English, German, Spanish, and French), with the system achieving top rankings in Spanish, English, and German using few-shot prompting with Gemini Pro and contextual information.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Multilingual Approach:** The paper's evaluation across multiple languages is a significant strength, highlighting the generalizability (or lack thereof) of the proposed methods.
    *   **RAG Integration:** The incorporation of Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) allows the team to assess the effectiveness of external knowledge in mitigating hallucinations.
    *   **Synthetic Data Generation:**  The creation and use of synthetic training data to overcome the lack of annotated data is a practical approach.
    *   **Comprehensive experimentation:** The inclusion of multiple models (XLM-RoBERTa, Gemini 1.5 Pro, Llama-3) and fine-tuning approaches allows for a robust comparison.
    *   **Clarity:** The paper is well-structured, explains the methodology clearly, and reports results in a relatively easy-to-understand manner.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Synthetic Data Quality:** The reliance on synthetic data raises concerns about the fidelity of the generated examples to real-world hallucinations.  The paper acknowledges this, but a more in-depth discussion of the limitations and potential biases introduced by the synthetic data is warranted.
    *   **Retrieval Module Limitations:**  Indexing only the English version of Wikipedia for multilingual queries limits the effectiveness of the RAG system for non-English languages.
    *   **Lack of Detailed Ablation Studies:** While the paper explores various configurations, more detailed ablation studies would be useful to understand the contribution of individual components (e.g., the effect of prompt engineering choices, different retrieval strategies, or the specific data used for fine-tuning).
    *   **Limited Novelty of Individual Components:** The individual components (RAG, prompt engineering, fine-tuning) are well-established techniques. The novelty primarily lies in their combination and application to the hallucination detection task.
    *   **Prompt Engineering:** Although the prompt engineering techniques were applied, it can be argued they were mostly limited to what other approaches perform, namely, adding in retrieved data.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The paper contributes to the growing body of research on hallucination detection in LLMs, a crucial area for ensuring the reliability and trustworthiness of these models.
    *   The combination of RAG, prompt engineering, and fine-tuning strategies represents a practical approach to the problem.
    *   The multilingual evaluation provides valuable insights into the cross-lingual transferability of hallucination detection techniques.
    *   The most significant contribution is the demonstration of top results through prompt engineering and RAG combination.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The paper could influence future research on hallucination detection by highlighting the importance of RAG and fine-tuning.
    *   The insights from the multilingual evaluation could guide the development of more robust and generalizable hallucination detection models.
    *   The use of synthetic data generation could inspire other researchers to explore this approach for similar tasks where labeled data is scarce.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a solid engineering effort with a focus on practical solutions for hallucination detection in QA systems. The strengths lie in the multilingual approach, integration of RAG, and the use of synthetic data. However, the limitations of the synthetic data, the relatively incremental novelty of the techniques, and retrieval limitations restrict the overall impact. The top ranks achieved in specific languages provides some value as well.

Score: 6.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Incident Response Planning Using a Lightweight Large Language Model with Reduced Hallucination
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05188v1)
- **Authors**: Kim Hammar, Tansu Alpcan, Emil C. Lupu
- **Abstract**: Timely and effective incident response is key to managing the growing frequency of cyberattacks. However, identifying the right response actions for complex systems is a major technical challenge. A promising approach to mitigate this challenge is to use the security knowledge embedded in large language models (LLMs) to assist security operators during incident handling. Recent research has demonstrated the potential of this approach, but current methods are mainly based on prompt engineering of frontier LLMs, which is costly and prone to hallucinations. We address these limitations by presenting a novel way to use an LLM for incident response planning with reduced hallucination. Our method includes three steps: fine-tuning, information retrieval, and lookahead planning. We prove that our method generates response plans with a bounded probability of hallucination and that this probability can be made arbitrarily small at the expense of increased planning time under certain assumptions. Moreover, we show that our method is lightweight and can run on commodity hardware. We evaluate our method on logs from incidents reported in the literature. The experimental results show that our method a) achieves up to 22% shorter recovery times than frontier LLMs and b) generalizes to a broad range of incident types and response actions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper introduces a novel method for incident response planning using a lightweight Large Language Model (LLM) with the explicit goal of reducing hallucinations. The method combines instruction fine-tuning, Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG), and decision-theoretic planning. The lightweight LLM is fine-tuned on a dataset of incident logs and corresponding response plans. RAG is used to augment the LLM's knowledge with real-time threat intelligence and system knowledge. Finally, decision-theoretic planning uses the LLM to generate multiple candidate response actions and selects the action leading to the shortest expected recovery time. The authors provide a theoretical analysis showing a probabilistic upper bound on the hallucination probability and demonstrate empirically that the method achieves better recovery times than frontier LLMs while requiring significantly fewer resources. They evaluate the method on logs from incidents reported in the literature and compare its performance to frontier LLMs and a reinforcement learning approach (PPO).

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its holistic approach to address the hallucination problem in LLM-driven incident response planning. While individual components like fine-tuning and RAG are established, the combination of these techniques with decision-theoretic planning, specifically designed to reduce hallucinations, represents a novel contribution. The derivation of a probabilistic upper bound on hallucinations adds a theoretical component not seen in related LLM-based security research. Creating and sharing a fine-tuned model and a dataset tailored for incident response is also a significant contribution to the community, fostering further research and development in this area. It is one of the very few LLM-based methods that are fully open-source.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its potential to make LLM-based incident response more practical and accessible. Frontier LLMs, while powerful, are resource-intensive and prone to hallucinations, hindering their real-world deployment. By using a lightweight LLM and explicitly addressing hallucinations, the paper paves the way for more efficient and reliable incident response planning. The empirical results demonstrate a tangible improvement in recovery times, suggesting that the proposed method can significantly aid security operators in mitigating cyberattacks. The fact that their method does not require incident-specific training gives it a major advantage over reinforcement learning techniques like PPO. The paper also releases a dataset and a fine-tuned model, which can become a valuable resource for the research community to build upon and advance the field of automated incident response.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Approach:** The paper provides a well-defined and integrated methodology combining fine-tuning, RAG, and decision-theoretic planning.
    *   **Hallucination Mitigation:** The paper tackles the critical problem of hallucinations in LLM-based systems, offering both theoretical analysis and practical techniques to reduce their impact.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper provides thorough experimental results comparing the proposed method with state-of-the-art LLMs and reinforcement learning methods on real-world incident data.
    *   **Resource Efficiency:** Using a lightweight LLM makes the method more accessible and deployable on commodity hardware.
    *   **Open Source Contribution:** Releasing the fine-tuned model, dataset, and source code promotes reproducibility and facilitates further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Simplified System Model:** The recovery state model, while based on MITRE D3FEND, is relatively simple and might not fully capture the complexity of real-world incident response scenarios.
    *   **Limited Evaluation Scenarios:** Although the evaluation includes different datasets, it would be beneficial to evaluate the method in a more realistic operational setting with human security operators.
    *   **Assumptions in Theoretical Analysis:** The theoretical analysis relies on specific assumptions, such as the knowledge of δ and η, which might not always hold in practice. The paper acknowledges this but further investigation of the robustness of the method under different conditions would be beneficial.
    *   **Scalability of Planning:** While the paper shows that parallelization can help, the planning component with M=3 samples still introduces computational overhead, which could become a bottleneck in highly dynamic environments. More sophisticated planning algorithms or techniques to reduce the sampling requirements could improve scalability.
    * The performance gains over frontier LLMs are modest and may not justify the added complexity of the framework.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to significantly influence the field by providing a practical and accessible approach to LLM-based incident response. It addresses a crucial limitation of existing methods (hallucinations) and offers a theoretically sound framework for mitigating this problem. The open-source nature of the work will likely stimulate further research and development in this area. As LLMs continue to evolve, the techniques presented in this paper could be adapted and extended to create more robust and intelligent security systems.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

The paper demonstrates significant novelty by holistically combining methods to address the critical issue of hallucination in LLM-driven incident response. The paper delivers tangible benefits, achieving better recovery times while using fewer resources. The open-source aspect fosters community engagement and further development.

However, the framework introduces computational costs and relies on simplifying assumptions, limiting its immediate applicability in all operational settings. Further investigation into the method's robustness and scalability would be beneficial. Additionally, the observed improvements, while present, are not revolutionary, warranting a somewhat conservative score. Nevertheless, the paper represents a solid contribution to the field and offers a promising direction for future research.

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### AI-assisted JSON Schema Creation and Mapping
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05192v1)
- **Authors**: Felix Neubauer, Jürgen Pleiss, Benjamin Uekermann
- **Abstract**: Model-Driven Engineering (MDE) places models at the core of system and data engineering processes. In the context of research data, these models are typically expressed as schemas that define the structure and semantics of datasets. However, many domains still lack standardized models, and creating them remains a significant barrier, especially for non-experts. We present a hybrid approach that combines large language models (LLMs) with deterministic techniques to enable JSON Schema creation, modification, and schema mapping based on natural language inputs by the user. These capabilities are integrated into the open-source tool MetaConfigurator, which already provides visual model editing, validation, code generation, and form generation from models. For data integration, we generate schema mappings from heterogeneous JSON, CSV, XML, and YAML data using LLMs, while ensuring scalability and reliability through deterministic execution of generated mapping rules. The applicability of our work is demonstrated in an application example in the field of chemistry. By combining natural language interaction with deterministic safeguards, this work significantly lowers the barrier to structured data modeling and data integration for non-experts.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "AI-assisted JSON Schema Creation and Mapping":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a hybrid approach combining Large Language Models (LLMs) with deterministic techniques to facilitate JSON Schema creation, modification, and mapping using natural language. This integration is implemented within the MetaConfigurator tool, which already provides visual schema editing, validation, and code generation features. The approach addresses limitations of solely relying on LLMs by incorporating prompt engineering, context management, post-processing safeguards, and visual feedback. For data integration, the system generates human-readable mapping rules (JSONata) from heterogeneous data sources (JSON, CSV, XML, YAML) using LLMs, followed by deterministic execution to ensure reliability and scalability. A chemistry application example demonstrates the practical applicability of the developed approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the integration of LLMs with a model-driven engineering (MDE) tool (MetaConfigurator) for JSON Schema tasks. While using LLMs for schema generation and data transformation isn't entirely new, the paper distinguishes itself by:

    *   **Hybrid approach:**  It avoids relying solely on LLMs and strategically combines their strengths with the control and predictability of MDE principles. The emphasis on deterministic execution of LLM-generated mapping rules is a key contribution.
    *   **Tool integration:** The integration within MetaConfigurator, an existing and extensible tool, provides a practical and immediately usable implementation, including visual feedback and schema validation which are not standard capabilities of straight LLM-based solutions.
    *   **Focus on human-in-the-loop:** The design explicitly supports human review and correction of LLM outputs, aligning with the need for human oversight in data integration, as suggested by Buss et al.

    However, the individual components (LLMs for schema inference and mapping, schema editors, JSONata) are not novel by themselves. The *combination* and integration within the existing MDE framework provides the novelty. Also, the paper does not introduce fundamentally new techniques in prompt engineering; the strategies it employs are established best practices.

*   **Significance:** The paper has significance because:

    *   **Lowers the barrier to entry:** It makes structured data modeling and data integration more accessible to non-experts by allowing them to interact using natural language. This can democratize MDE techniques, enabling wider adoption in domains where data models are lacking.
    *   **Addresses LLM shortcomings:**  The hybrid approach directly tackles the limitations of LLMs, such as lack of guaranteed validity, interpretability issues, and scalability challenges. This makes the overall system more reliable and trustworthy.
    *   **Practical application:** The chemistry example demonstrates a real-world use case and showcases the benefits of the approach in transforming unstructured data into structured, AI-ready representations.
    *   **Open-source implementation:**  The fact that the integration is part of an open-source tool (MetaConfigurator) promotes wider adoption and further research.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem statement and well-defined objectives.
    *   A hybrid approach that strategically leverages the strengths of both LLMs and MDE.
    *   Practical implementation within an existing tool.
    *   A real-world example demonstrating applicability.
    *   The focus on validation and human review ensures correctness of the schema and transformations.
    *   Deterministic execution of LLM-generated mapping provides robustness and scalability.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited evaluation:** The evaluation is centered on a single application example. A more comprehensive evaluation involving different domains and datasets would strengthen the paper's claims. While the chemistry example is described well, its impact on the reproducibility in the chemistry domain is not discussed, meaning how much the overall process can be accelerated.
    *   **Lack of comparison:**  The paper lacks a quantitative comparison against other schema mapping or generation approaches (e.g., purely LLM-based or rule-based methods) to demonstrate the superiority of the hybrid approach.
    *   **Limited scalability evaluation:** The paper claims to ensure scalability. However, no data is reported about performance in terms of processing larger datasets.
    *   **JSONata dependency:** Reliance on JSONata limits the complexity and types of transformations that can be achieved, potentially requiring custom code for advanced scenarios.
    *   **Unclear supported document complexity** Due to including the inferred schema of the input document in the LLM prompt, an upper boundary on document complexity is supported. However, how complex a JSON document can be handled is unclear.

**Score:** 7/10

**Justification:**

The paper presents a valuable and practical approach to integrating LLMs into the model-driven engineering workflow for JSON Schema creation and mapping. The integration within MetaConfigurator provides a usable and extensible platform. However, the lack of a comprehensive evaluation including different domains, performance data on large-scale datasets, and a comparison with existing methods limits the score. While the conceptual integration is strong, more empirical evidence is needed to fully validate the paper's claims and assess its impact. The approach is novel within the context of MDE and has the potential to significantly impact how non-experts engage with structured data modeling. The emphasis on a hybrid approach with deterministic execution is a key differentiator from other works.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### STEPWISE-CODEX-Bench: Evaluating Complex Multi-Function Comprehension and Fine-Grained Execution Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05193v1)
- **Authors**: Kaiwen Yan, Yuhang Chang, Zirui Guo, Yaling Mou, Jiang Ming, Jingwei Sun
- **Abstract**: In recent years, large language models (LLMs) have made significant progress in code intelligence, yet systematically evaluating their code understanding and reasoning abilities remains challenging. Mainstream benchmarks such as HumanEval and MBPP primarily assess functional correctness, while reasoning benchmarks like CRUXEVAL are limited to single-function, low-complexity scenarios. As a result, advanced models achieve nearly saturated scores, limiting their discriminative power. To address this, we present STEPWISE-CODEX-Bench (SX-Bench), a novel benchmark designed for complex multi-function understanding and fine-grained execution reasoning. SX-Bench features tasks involving collaboration among multiple sub-functions (e.g., chained calls, nested loops), shifting evaluation towards overall control and data flow modeling. It defines "computation steps" as the minimal execution unit and requires models to predict the total number of steps in reasoning tasks, thereby assessing a model's in-depth understanding of dynamic execution beyond simple I/O matching. Evaluation on over 20 mainstream models (including 14 reasoning-enhanced models) demonstrates that SX-Bench is highly discriminative: even the state-of-the-art OpenAI-O3 achieves only 78.37 percent accuracy on Hard-Reasoning tasks, much lower than its saturated scores on previous benchmarks, thereby revealing bottlenecks in complex and fine-grained reasoning. We also release an automated pipeline combining program synthesis, symbolic execution, and LLM-aided validation for efficient benchmark generation and quality assurance. SX-Bench advances code evaluation from "single-function verification" to "multi-function dynamic reasoning," providing a key tool for the in-depth assessment of advanced code intelligence models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "STEPWISE-CODEX-Bench: Evaluating Complex Multi-Function Comprehension and Fine-Grained Execution Reasoning":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces STEPWISE-CODEX-Bench (SX-Bench), a new benchmark for evaluating the code understanding and reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs).  It addresses the limitations of existing benchmarks, which primarily focus on functional correctness of code *generation* and lack the complexity to thoroughly assess models' *comprehension* of code execution. SX-Bench emphasizes multi-function collaboration, control/data flow modeling, and fine-grained execution reasoning by defining computation steps and requiring models to predict their number during program execution. The authors evaluated over 20 models, including reasoning-enhanced models, and found SX-Bench to have strong discriminative power. They also developed an automated task generation and quality assurance pipeline to support the creation of complex benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a Crucial Gap:**  SX-Bench tackles a significant shortcoming in the LLM evaluation landscape.  Current benchmarks are indeed saturating, offering little insight into models' *understanding* of complex code rather than simply their ability to generate correct code snippets.  This is particularly relevant for complex tasks like debugging, code optimization, and understanding legacy code.
    *   **Well-Defined Evaluation Paradigm:**  The "stepwise execution tracing" paradigm, using the concept of "computation steps", is a novel and potentially effective way to assess fine-grained reasoning.  It moves beyond simple I/O matching and forces models to simulate program execution.
    *   **Multi-Function Complexity:**  The focus on multi-function collaboration with complex control/data flow is a strong departure from existing benchmarks like HumanEval and CRUXEVAL, which are mainly focused on single functions. This better mirrors real-world code.
    *   **Automated Task Generation:** The development of an automated task generation and quality assurance pipeline is a key strength, enabling the creation of a large and diverse benchmark without excessive manual effort. The integration of program synthesis, symbolic execution, and LLM-aided verification are great and welcome features.
    *   **Strong Experimental Evaluation:** The thorough evaluation of a wide range of models (including both general LLMs and reasoning-enhanced variants) highlights the limitations of current models and the discriminative power of SX-Bench. The breakdown of the analysis by subset (Predict, Easy-Reasoning, Hard-Reasoning) is insightful.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** While the benchmark tackles multi-function collaboration, the underlying functions still appear relatively simple (arithmetic, logical, string manipulation).  Future work could explore more complex operations or even integration with external libraries.
    *   **Artificial Tasks:**  While the benchmark construction methods are admirable, the inherently synthetic nature of the generated code means that performance on SX-Bench may not perfectly correlate with real-world code understanding.
    *   **Evaluation Metric:** While "Accuracy" is easy to understand, the requirement to get ALL <input, output> pairs correct for a sample to be deemed correct may be overly stringent. Partial credit, or alternative metrics could provide a more nuanced view of model performance.
    *   **Lack of Comparison with Other Reasoning Methods:** The paper focuses primarily on improving model capabilities for code comprehension, and then it analyzes that capability on the code generation task. A comparison against other methods and related works that focus directly on code reasoning would add valuable context.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    The novelty lies in the specific combination of multi-function complexity, stepwise execution tracing, and automated task generation. Existing benchmarks focus on either code *generation* or single-function *reasoning*. SX-Bench represents a significant step towards more comprehensive evaluation of code understanding. The automated generation also enhances scalability.

    The significance of the work lies in providing a much-needed tool for researchers to better understand the strengths and weaknesses of LLMs when it comes to code.  By exposing limitations in complex code reasoning, it can guide future research towards developing more capable and robust code intelligence systems. The design and release of the benchmark itself is a valuable contribution to the community.

**Justification for the Score:**

SX-Bench is a solid contribution to the field. While it has some limitations in terms of scope and task complexity (being still somewhat synthetic), it effectively highlights the weaknesses of existing LLMs in a way that existing benchmarks don't. The evaluation is thorough and the benchmark is publicly available, encouraging further research in this direction.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### QA-Dragon: Query-Aware Dynamic RAG System for Knowledge-Intensive Visual Question Answering
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05197v1)
- **Authors**: Zhuohang Jiang, Pangjing Wu, Xu Yuan, Wenqi Fan, Qing Li
- **Abstract**: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) has been introduced to mitigate hallucinations in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) by incorporating external knowledge into the generation process, and it has become a widely adopted approach for knowledge-intensive Visual Question Answering (VQA). However, existing RAG methods typically retrieve from either text or images in isolation, limiting their ability to address complex queries that require multi-hop reasoning or up-to-date factual knowledge. To address this limitation, we propose QA-Dragon, a Query-Aware Dynamic RAG System for Knowledge-Intensive VQA. Specifically, QA-Dragon introduces a domain router to identify the query's subject domain for domain-specific reasoning, along with a search router that dynamically selects optimal retrieval strategies. By orchestrating both text and image search agents in a hybrid setup, our system supports multimodal, multi-turn, and multi-hop reasoning, enabling it to tackle complex VQA tasks effectively. We evaluate our QA-Dragon on the Meta CRAG-MM Challenge at KDD Cup 2025, where it significantly enhances the reasoning performance of base models under challenging scenarios. Our framework achieves substantial improvements in both answer accuracy and knowledge overlap scores, outperforming baselines by 5.06% on the single-source task, 6.35% on the multi-source task, and 5.03% on the multi-turn task.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces QA-Dragon, a Query-Aware Dynamic Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) system designed for knowledge-intensive Visual Question Answering (VQA).  QA-Dragon addresses limitations in existing RAG methods that retrieve information from text or images in isolation. The system incorporates a domain router, a search router, and specialized retrieval agents (text and image) to support multimodal, multi-turn, and multi-hop reasoning. The system dynamically selects retrieval strategies based on query characteristics and integrates a multimodal reranker to ensure the relevance and quality of retrieved information. Evaluations on the Meta CRAG-MM Challenge demonstrate significant improvements in answer accuracy and knowledge overlap compared to baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a well-integrated RAG system with several components contributing to improved VQA performance. The query-aware dynamic routing using domain and search routers is a significant element of novelty, allowing for adaptation to different query types and reducing reliance on any single retrieval strategy. The integration of multimodal information with a two-stage reranker and post-answer verification also contributes to the novelty of the work.

*   **Significance:** The results demonstrate substantial improvements over baseline methods on a challenging real-world dataset. The performance gains on the single-source, multi-source, and multi-turn tasks suggest that the proposed system addresses critical limitations of existing RAG approaches for VQA, and is potentially significant within the field.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive System Design:** QA-Dragon incorporates multiple modular components designed to enhance the RAG pipeline specifically for VQA.
    *   **Dynamic Query Routing:**  The domain and search routers allow for adaptive information retrieval based on query characteristics.
    *   **Significant Empirical Results:**  The improvements in accuracy and knowledge overlap scores demonstrate the effectiveness of the approach.
    *   **Real-World Dataset:** Evaluation on the CRAG-MM Challenge, a benchmark designed to emulate real-world conditions, strengthens the results.
    *   **Ablation study:** Highlights the impact of specific system components.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Complexity:** The system has many components, and the reliance on various pre-trained language models introduces complexity. The computational overhead might be a limitation. The increased latency is also evident.
    *   **Task specific fine-tuning:** Task specific dataset required, the general applicability may be limited.
    *   **Limited Generality beyond VQA:** While effective for VQA, the architecture may require modifications for other tasks.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's emphasis on query-aware routing and multimodal integration in RAG could influence future research on VQA and related tasks. The use of explicit reasoning traces for retrieval strategy selection is also a promising direction.

**Justification of Score:**

The QA-Dragon paper represents a well-engineered and effectively implemented RAG system for VQA with meaningful contributions to the field. The novelty lies in the integrated design and the dynamic routing components, leading to impressive performance gains on a challenging dataset. While the complexity of the system is a concern and limits generality, the empirical results and potential influence on subsequent research justify a score of:

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### EvoGraph: Hybrid Directed Graph Evolution toward Software 3.0
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05199v1)
- **Authors**: Igor Costa, Christopher Baran
- **Abstract**: We introduce **EvoGraph**, a framework that enables software systems to evolve their own source code, build pipelines, documentation, and tickets. EvoGraph represents every artefact in a typed directed graph, applies learned mutation operators driven by specialized small language models (SLMs), and selects survivors with a multi-objective fitness. On three benchmarks, EvoGraph fixes 83% of known security vulnerabilities, translates COBOL to Java with 93% functional equivalence (test verified), and maintains documentation freshness within two minutes. Experiments show a 40% latency reduction and a sevenfold drop in feature lead time compared with strong baselines. We extend our approach to **evoGraph**, leveraging language-specific SLMs for modernizing .NET, Lisp, CGI, ColdFusion, legacy Python, and C codebases, achieving 82-96% semantic equivalence across languages while reducing computational costs by 90% compared to large language models. EvoGraph's design responds to empirical failure modes in legacy modernization, such as implicit contracts, performance preservation, and integration evolution. Our results suggest a practical path toward Software 3.0, where systems adapt continuously yet remain under measurable control.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces EvoGraph, a framework for automating the evolution of software systems.  EvoGraph uses a hybrid directed graph to represent software artifacts (code, documentation, build files, etc.) and applies learned mutation operators powered by specialized Small Language Models (SLMs). A multi-objective fitness function and a safety gate are used to select improvements while adhering to functional, performance, security, and integration constraints. The authors evaluate EvoGraph on benchmarks including fixing security vulnerabilities, translating legacy code (COBOL to Java), and maintaining documentation freshness. They demonstrate significant improvements in latency reduction, feature lead time, and a reduction in computational costs compared to using larger language models (LLMs). The authors emphasize the importance of using SLMs for specialized tasks in agentic systems and show how EvoGraph is aligned with industry best practices for legacy modernization.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the *integration* of several existing techniques into a cohesive framework.  While SLMs are increasingly used for code generation and related tasks, the comprehensive graph-based representation of a software system's entire ecosystem (code, docs, build, tickets, etc.), combined with SLM-driven mutations, multi-objective optimization, and safety gates is a novel approach. The specific adaptations of evolutionary algorithms and automated program repair techniques within the evoGraph framework also contributes to the paper's novelty.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant problem: the high failure rate and cost of legacy software modernization. By automating the evolution of these systems, EvoGraph has the potential to reduce costs, accelerate development, and improve the quality of legacy code. The emphasis on safety is particularly important given the mission-critical nature of many legacy systems. The use of SLMs to achieve better efficiency and specialization is a significant contribution and aligns with the trends of agentic AI.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive approach:** EvoGraph considers all aspects of the software system, not just the code.
    *   **Safety-first design:** The safety gate ensures that changes do not break existing functionality or introduce new vulnerabilities.
    *   **Use of SLMs:** The authors leverage SLMs for better efficiency and specialization.
    *   **Empirical validation:** The paper includes experiments on realistic benchmarks, showing promising results.
    *   **Addresses practical challenges:** The framework's design takes into account empirical failure modes of legacy modernization.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dependency on SLM training data:** The performance of EvoGraph depends on the availability of training data for each legacy language. While the authors mention training data, the specific details and datasets used are not fully elaborated which makes it difficult to judge the replicability of the work. The process for creating or adapting training datasets is crucial for evaluating EvoGraph's applicability.
    *   **Scalability:** The paper mentions that scaling to large codebases (over a million LOC) requires sharding. More discussion on sharding strategies and potential performance implications would be helpful.
    *   **Generalizability:** The benchmarks used are limited in scope. It is not clear how well EvoGraph would perform on other types of legacy systems or with different types of modernization goals. Are the benchmarks sufficiently representative? A more in-depth discussion of benchmark selection and limitations would improve the paper.
    *   **Limited comparison to existing automated tooling**: Whilst there are mentions of comparisons against industry tooling, there are little concrete metrics.

**Score:** 7.

**Justification:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution by offering a novel framework that integrates several concepts effectively. The focus on safe, incremental software evolution for legacy systems, using SLMs and a directed graph approach, is promising and addresses a pressing industry need. However, the paper is limited by a lack of detailed information regarding crucial aspects of training data, generalizability, and scalability. Whilst the authors have shown that their approach results in improved computational costs, this does not necessarily translate into a practical application. In addition, the evaluation could have been more thorough. Further development and evaluation on diverse legacy systems are needed to validate the broad applicability of EvoGraph. These aspects, while addressed, need further clarification to increase the paper's score. The strengths of the paper, however, outweigh its weaknesses, justifying a score of 7.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### FAITH: A Framework for Assessing Intrinsic Tabular Hallucinations in finance
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05201v1)
- **Authors**: Mengao Zhang, Jiayu Fu, Tanya Warrier, Yuwen Wang, Tianhui Tan, Ke-wei Huang
- **Abstract**: Hallucination remains a critical challenge for deploying Large Language Models (LLMs) in finance. Accurate extraction and precise calculation from tabular data are essential for reliable financial analysis, since even minor numerical errors can undermine decision-making and regulatory compliance. Financial applications have unique requirements, often relying on context-dependent, numerical, and proprietary tabular data that existing hallucination benchmarks rarely capture. In this study, we develop a rigorous and scalable framework for evaluating intrinsic hallucinations in financial LLMs, conceptualized as a context-aware masked span prediction task over real-world financial documents. Our main contributions are: (1) a novel, automated dataset creation paradigm using a masking strategy; (2) a new hallucination evaluation dataset derived from S&P 500 annual reports; and (3) a comprehensive evaluation of intrinsic hallucination patterns in state-of-the-art LLMs on financial tabular data. Our work provides a robust methodology for in-house LLM evaluation and serves as a critical step toward building more trustworthy and reliable financial Generative AI systems.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, including a novelty/significance score and detailed justification:

**Summary:**

The paper "FAITH: A Framework for Assessing Intrinsic Tabular Hallucinations in Finance" addresses the critical challenge of hallucination in Large Language Models (LLMs) when applied to financial tabular data. The authors argue that existing hallucination benchmarks are inadequate for finance because they don't capture the specific complexities of financial data, such as context dependency, numerical grounding, and the need for specialized reasoning. To address this gap, the paper introduces FAITH, a novel framework for evaluating intrinsic hallucinations in financial LLMs.  The framework's key components include: (1) an automated dataset creation paradigm using a masking strategy applied to real-world financial documents (S&P 500 annual reports), (2) a new hallucination evaluation dataset derived from these reports, and (3) a comprehensive evaluation of state-of-the-art LLMs on financial tabular data, with a breakdown by reasoning type (Direct Lookup, Comparative, Bivariate, and Multivariate Calculation). The paper quantifies the extent of hallucinations and provides insights into how they are affected by task complexity.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
