    *   **Addressing a Critical Gap:** The paper tackles a significant and timely problem. The financial industry's increasing reliance on LLMs necessitates robust methods for assessing and mitigating the risk of hallucination. Existing benchmarks largely ignore the nuances of financial data.
    *   **Novelty in Dataset Creation:** The automated dataset creation paradigm using a masking strategy is a notable contribution. It allows for scalable construction of evaluation datasets, reducing reliance on manual annotation, which is especially relevant for proprietary financial data. This approach to automatic test data generation is novel and scalable.
    *   **Context-Awareness and Reasoning Complexity:** The focus on intrinsic hallucinations within a specific context (financial documents) is crucial. The breakdown by reasoning type (Direct Lookup, Comparative, Bivariate, and Multivariate) allows for a fine-grained analysis of where LLMs struggle.
    *   **Real-World Relevance:** The use of S&P 500 annual reports as a data source enhances the real-world relevance and practical applicability of the framework.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation and Analysis:** The thorough evaluation of various LLMs, with a detailed analysis of error patterns and a case study, provides valuable insights for researchers and practitioners.
    *   **Quantifiable Hierarchy of Model Reliability:** The work shows the different models perform in quantifiable levels, which is helpful to the financial industry and users of the LLMs to consider these factors.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Dataset Scope:** While using S&P 500 annual reports adds value, the dataset may not fully represent the diversity of financial documents and tasks (e.g., regulatory filings, credit reports, transaction data, customer data).
    *   **Reliance on LLMs for Annotation:** Although the pilot study demonstrates the reliability of LLM-based answerability annotation, the potential for bias remains a concern. Error introduced during the step would undermine the results.
    *   **Limited Mitigation Strategies:** The paper focuses primarily on evaluation. While valuable, it would be even stronger if it included preliminary exploration of mitigation strategies for the identified hallucination patterns.
    *   **Generalizability concerns:** The evaluation uses very recent annual reports from 2024 from S&P 500 companies in the U.S. The models might perform very differently on other countries' annual reports, or other types of documents (or even on reports from a prior year).

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper is likely to have a significant impact on the development and deployment of LLMs in the financial industry.
    *   The FAITH framework provides a valuable tool for evaluating and comparing LLMs in a financial context.
    *   The insights into hallucination patterns can inform the development of more robust LLMs and effective mitigation strategies.
    *   The automated dataset creation paradigm has the potential to be adapted to other domains with specialized data requirements.
    *   The paper can serve as a benchmark to evaluate the performance of various models on financial data and allow progress to be tracked.

*   **Novelty:** The paper exhibits a good degree of novelty. The combination of automated dataset creation, context-aware evaluation, financial domain focus, and breakdown by reasoning complexity is a significant advancement over existing hallucination benchmarks.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **8** to this paper.

*   The paper addresses a very important problem with a practical solution.
*   The proposed automated evaluation dataset generation is a strong contribution.
*   While the paper has some limitations, the strengths outweigh the weaknesses.
*   The paper has the potential to significantly influence the development and deployment of trustworthy LLMs in the finance sector.
*   The dataset will be of value to other research teams.

The work addresses a critical gap in the LLM evaluation landscape, and the approach will be of interest to many research and applied teams.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### SPEX: A Vision-Language Model for Land Cover Extraction on Spectral Remote Sensing Images
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05202v1)
- **Authors**: Dongchen Si, Di Wang, Erzhong Gao, Xiaolei Qin, Liu Zhao, Jing Zhang, Minqiang Xu, Jianbo Zhan, Jianshe Wang, Lin Liu, Bo Du, Liangpei Zhang
- **Abstract**: Spectral information has long been recognized as a critical cue in remote sensing observations. Although numerous vision-language models have been developed for pixel-level interpretation, spectral information remains underutilized, resulting in suboptimal performance, particularly in multispectral scenarios. To address this limitation, we construct a vision-language instruction-following dataset named SPIE, which encodes spectral priors of land-cover objects into textual attributes recognizable by large language models (LLMs), based on classical spectral index computations. Leveraging this dataset, we propose SPEX, a multimodal LLM designed for instruction-driven land cover extraction. To this end, we introduce several carefully designed components and training strategies, including multiscale feature aggregation, token context condensation, and multispectral visual pre-training, to achieve precise and flexible pixel-level interpretation. To the best of our knowledge, SPEX is the first multimodal vision-language model dedicated to land cover extraction in spectral remote sensing imagery. Extensive experiments on five public multispectral datasets demonstrate that SPEX consistently outperforms existing state-of-the-art methods in extracting typical land cover categories such as vegetation, buildings, and water bodies. Moreover, SPEX is capable of generating textual explanations for its predictions, thereby enhancing interpretability and user-friendliness. Code will be released at: https://github.com/MiliLab/SPEX.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "SPEX: A Vision-Language Model for Land Cover Extraction on Spectral Remote Sensing Images":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SPEX, a novel vision-language model (VLM) designed for instruction-driven land cover extraction from multispectral remote sensing imagery. SPEX aims to address the limitations of existing VLMs that don't fully utilize spectral information, a crucial aspect of remote sensing data. The key contributions include:

1.  **SPIE Dataset:** A new dataset called Spectral Prompt Instruction Extraction (SPIE) is constructed. This dataset encodes spectral priors (information derived from the relationships between spectral bands) of land cover objects into textual attributes recognizable by large language models (LLMs).
2.  **SPEX Model Architecture:** The SPEX architecture combines a vision encoder, an LLM, and a lightweight decoder, enhanced with multiscale feature aggregation and token context condensation, to enable pixel-level interpretation.
3.  **Multispectral Visual Pre-training:** The vision encoder is pre-trained on multispectral imagery to better adapt to the domain.
4.  **Instruction-Driven Extraction:** SPEX can perform land cover extraction based on textual instructions, allowing for flexible and interactive interpretation.
5.  **Performance:** Experiments on five public multispectral datasets demonstrate that SPEX outperforms state-of-the-art methods in extracting common land cover categories. It can also generate textual explanations for its predictions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Dataset:** The SPIE dataset is a key novel contribution. Encoding spectral priors into textual prompts is an interesting and potentially impactful idea. The auxiliary instruction design for response generation further enhances the quality of the dataset.
*   **Model Architecture:** While the individual components (vision encoder, LLM, SAM decoder) are not entirely new, the specific combination and the additions of the MSAM and TCP modules, tailored for spectral remote sensing data, contribute to the novelty. The MSAM module specifically seems well-motivated to extract information from the vision encoder, and the TCP module is crucial for creating an understandable output for the model's SAM decoder.
*   **Multispectral Pre-training:** The specific visual pre-training strategy helps to align the data to the remote sensing domain, which is essential in order to increase the model's reliability in interpreting satellite images.

**Significance:**

*   **Addressing Spectral Information Gap:** SPEX directly tackles the underutilization of spectral information in existing VLMs for remote sensing. This is a critical aspect, as spectral information is a primary source of information in identifying specific classes of land covers.
*   **Improved Performance:** The paper presents strong experimental results across multiple datasets, showing consistent improvement over existing state-of-the-art methods. This is the most significant claim, and the paper needs sufficient quantitative results, as provided.
*   **Interpretability and User-Friendliness:** The ability to generate textual explanations and follow instructions enhances the interpretability and usability of the model for non-expert users.
*   **Generalizability:** The experiments were performed on 5 datasets which helps with demonstrating the generalizability of the results.

**Strengths:**

*   Clear and well-structured paper.
*   Well-motivated problem and approach.
*   Strong experimental results and comparisons.
*   Novel dataset and model architecture.
*   Addresses a specific and important gap in the field.
*   The text explanations are a good addition to a useful project.
*   Detailed ablation studies prove the effectiveness of the models.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Component-level Novelty:** The core building blocks (LLM, vision encoder, SAM) are pre-existing. The novelty lies primarily in the design and training strategy.
*   **Dataset Bias:** As with any dataset, there is potential for bias within the SPIE dataset. The paper could include a discussion of any potential biases and their implications.
*   **Computational Cost:** The paper does not clearly state any costs associated with the model itself.

**Justification for the Score:**

The SPEX paper represents a **significant** contribution to the field of remote sensing and vision-language modeling. The novel SPIE dataset, specifically encoding spectral prior information, addresses a key limitation in current VLMs for remote sensing. The model architecture and training strategy are well-designed and lead to substantial performance improvements. While the individual components are not entirely novel, the creative combination and adaptations to the spectral remote sensing domain demonstrate significant engineering effort. The ability to generate textual explanations also adds value. The work has demonstrated significant strengths and high generalizability in a variety of data environments.

Despite relying on existing models and datasets, the specific training, data preparation, and architecture of the SPEX model, combined with the novelty of spectral modeling within an LLM, indicates a substantial contribution to the field. The potential impact on various applications related to land cover monitoring and analysis is considerable. Therefore, I award a score of **8**.
Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### ReasoningTrack: Chain-of-Thought Reasoning for Long-term Vision-Language Tracking
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05221v1)
- **Authors**: Xiao Wang, Liye Jin, Xufeng Lou, Shiao Wang, Lan Chen, Bo Jiang, Zhipeng Zhang
- **Abstract**: Vision-language tracking has received increasing attention in recent years, as textual information can effectively address the inflexibility and inaccuracy associated with specifying the target object to be tracked. Existing works either directly fuse the fixed language with vision features or simply modify using attention, however, their performance is still limited. Recently, some researchers have explored using text generation to adapt to the variations in the target during tracking, however, these works fail to provide insights into the model's reasoning process and do not fully leverage the advantages of large models, which further limits their overall performance. To address the aforementioned issues, this paper proposes a novel reasoning-based vision-language tracking framework, named ReasoningTrack, based on a pre-trained vision-language model Qwen2.5-VL. Both SFT (Supervised Fine-Tuning) and reinforcement learning GRPO are used for the optimization of reasoning and language generation. We embed the updated language descriptions and feed them into a unified tracking backbone network together with vision features. Then, we adopt a tracking head to predict the specific location of the target object. In addition, we propose a large-scale long-term vision-language tracking benchmark dataset, termed TNLLT, which contains 200 video sequences. 20 baseline visual trackers are re-trained and evaluated on this dataset, which builds a solid foundation for the vision-language visual tracking task. Extensive experiments on multiple vision-language tracking benchmark datasets fully validated the effectiveness of our proposed reasoning-based natural language generation strategy. The source code of this paper will be released on https://github.com/Event-AHU/Open_VLTrack
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary, a critical evaluation of the novelty, significance, and a score with justification.

**Summary:**

The paper "ReasoningTrack: Chain-of-Thought Reasoning for Long-term Vision-Language Tracking" introduces a novel framework for vision-language tracking that leverages chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning. It addresses the limitations of existing methods that either use static language descriptions or update them directly without a reasoning process. The ReasoningTrack framework uses a pre-trained vision-language model (Qwen2.5-VL) to dynamically update language descriptions with a reasoning chain. The framework is trained using both supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and reinforcement learning (GRPO). It combines updated language descriptions with visual features in a unified tracking backbone. Additionally, the paper introduces a new large-scale long-term vision-language tracking benchmark dataset, TNLLT, containing 200 video sequences. Experiments on multiple benchmark datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed reasoning-based language generation strategy.

**Critical Evaluation of Novelty and Significance:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel approach by integrating chain-of-thought reasoning into vision-language tracking. Using a pre-trained VLM to monitor tracking and dynamically update language descriptions with a reasoning process is a significant departure from existing methods. This contrasts with simpler adaptation strategies or static language usage. The proposed TNLLT dataset is another contribution, specifically addressing the need for long-term video data in this area. While some works have previously attempted to adapt text for visual tracking, the explicitly reasoning-driven approach is a novel aspect that could enhance robustness and interpretability.

*   **Significance:** The work has the potential to influence the field of vision-language tracking. By enabling the model to reason about the target object's changes over time, the approach can better handle challenges like occlusions, appearance variations, and changes in viewpoint, thereby improving tracking robustness. The introduction of TNLLT dataset is another valuable contribution as it provides the community with resources and benchmark. This new dataset can also facilitate the development of better tracking algorithms.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Reasoning-based approach:** The integration of CoT reasoning is a strong point, providing interpretability and robustness in language updates.
    *   **Unified framework:** Combining language and vision features in a unified tracking backbone is advantageous for efficient feature extraction and interaction.
    *   **Large-scale dataset:** The introduction of TNLLT fills a gap in the field by providing a large-scale benchmark for long-term vision-language tracking.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:** The experiments on multiple benchmark datasets demonstrates that this is a working method.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational cost:** The integration of a large pre-trained model makes the tracking speed insufficient for real-time applications.
    *   **Fixed-interval update:** The text update strategy has a fixed-interval, which prevents adaptation to rapid appearance changes.
    *   **Dependency on Pre-trained Models:** The success of this approach heavily relies on the pre-trained language model, so it has potential dependencies.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a significant contribution to the vision-language tracking field, mainly due to the novel reasoning-driven approach, which improves robustness and interpretability. The TNLLT dataset fills a critical gap in resources. The experiments confirm performance improvements. While the method has limitations in computational cost and text update strategy, its potential for improving tracking performance is substantial.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Cross-LoRA: A Data-Free LoRA Transfer Framework across Heterogeneous LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05232v1)
- **Authors**: Feifan Xia, Mingyang Liao, Yuyang Fang, Defang Li, Yantong Xie, Weikang Li, Yang Li, Deguo Xia, Jizhou Huang
- **Abstract**: Traditional parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) methods such as LoRA are tightly coupled with the base model architecture, which constrains their applicability across heterogeneous pretrained large language models (LLMs). To address this limitation, we introduce Cross-LoRA, a data-free framework for transferring LoRA modules between diverse base models without requiring additional training data. Cross-LoRA consists of two key components: (a) LoRA-Align, which performs subspace alignment between source and target base models through rank-truncated singular value decomposition (SVD) and Frobenius-optimal linear transformation, ensuring compatibility under dimension mismatch; and (b) LoRA-Shift, which applies the aligned subspaces to project source LoRA weight updates into the target model parameter space. Both components are data-free, training-free, and enable lightweight adaptation on a commodity GPU in 20 minutes. Experiments on ARCs, OBOA and HellaSwag show that Cross-LoRA achieves relative gains of up to 5.26% over base models. Across other commonsense reasoning benchmarks, Cross-LoRA maintains performance comparable to that of directly trained LoRA adapters.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Cross-LoRA, a data-free framework for transferring Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) modules between heterogeneous large language models (LLMs).  It addresses the limitation of LoRA adapters being tightly coupled to their base model architecture, making them unusable across different LLMs. Cross-LoRA consists of two main components: LoRA-Align, which aligns subspaces between source and target models using rank-truncated SVD and Frobenius-optimal linear transformation; and LoRA-Shift, which projects source LoRA weight updates into the target model's parameter space. The framework is data-free, training-free, and can be executed quickly on commodity hardware. Experiments on several datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of Cross-LoRA, achieving comparable performance to directly trained LoRA adapters and outperforming base models. The paper also analyzes the impact of subspace alignment and the transferability across different model architectures.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The idea of transferring LoRA adapters between heterogeneous LLMs is valuable.  While prior work (TransLoRA, LoRASuite, LoRA-X, ProLoRA) addresses PEFT transfer, they often rely on proxy data, training, are limited to specific architectures, or are domain-specific (diffusion models). Cross-LoRA's strength lies in its truly data-free and training-free approach and its focus on general LLMs. The use of SVD and Frobenius-optimal alignment isn't entirely new (SVD is commonly used for dimensionality reduction), but its application within this context, specifically for LoRA transfer between heterogeneous LLMs, appears novel. LoRA-Shift's projection technique, while relatively straightforward, is a crucial component that complements the alignment.

* **Significance:** The significance lies in its potential to reduce the cost and effort associated with adapting LLMs to new tasks. The ability to reuse pre-trained LoRA adapters across different models, especially as models are updated or replaced, offers a practical advantage. Furthermore, the data-free nature overcomes a major hurdle: access to the original fine-tuning dataset is not required. This greatly expands the applicability of LoRA-based fine-tuning, making it more efficient and accessible. The demonstration of transferability across different model families (Qwen, LLaMA, Gemma) increases its real-world applicability.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Data-free and Training-free:** A significant advantage over existing methods.
    * **General Applicability:**  The architecture-agnostic nature of Cross-LoRA is a major strength.
    * **Efficiency:** The framework's ability to operate on commodity GPUs in a relatively short time is highly practical.
    * **Empirical Validation:** The paper includes a solid set of experiments on relevant datasets.
    * **Clear Explanation:** The method and its components are well-described.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Performance Gaps:** While comparable to directly trained LoRA, Cross-LoRA doesn't *always* match its performance, especially on certain tasks. The paper acknowledges that architectural mismatches can limit transferability. The gap in performance on HellaSwag could suggest limitations in the approach's ability to transfer more complex reasoning skills.
    * **Limited Theoretical Analysis:** While the mathematical formulation is clear, a more in-depth theoretical analysis of the projection error and its relation to downstream performance would strengthen the paper. It's still largely an empirical study.
    * **Scalability Concerns:** While the experiments use decent-sized models, scaling up to the very largest models (100B+ parameters) and more complex tasks could reveal new challenges not seen in the current experiments.
   * **Limited Ablation Studies:** While there is one ablation study, further experiments isolating the effect of different components of the LoRA-Align module may be informative for improving the technique in the future.

* **Potential Impact:** This paper has the potential to influence the field by providing a viable approach to transferring knowledge across different LLMs without requiring extensive retraining or access to original data. It encourages the modularization and reuse of LoRA adapters, potentially leading to more efficient LLM development workflows. It may also spark further research into techniques for aligning model subspaces and transferring knowledge between heterogeneous architectures.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of 7.  The paper presents a genuinely useful and novel approach to LoRA transfer that addresses a significant limitation in the field. Its data-free and training-free nature is a major advantage, and the experimental results are promising. The implementation details are clear and reproducible.

However, the paper isn't perfect. While it offers a practical solution, the performance gap with directly trained LoRA and some limitations, alongside the potential scalability concerns and limited theoretical analysis and ablation studies prevent it from receiving a higher score. The paper is not a revolutionary breakthrough, but a significant and practical advancement in the area of parameter-efficient transfer learning.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Resource-Limited Joint Multimodal Sentiment Reasoning and Classification via Chain-of-Thought Enhancement and Distillation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05234v1)
- **Authors**: Haonan Shangguan, Xiaocui Yang, Shi Feng, Daling Wang, Yifei Zhang, Ge Yu
- **Abstract**: The surge in rich multimodal content on social media platforms has greatly advanced Multimodal Sentiment Analysis (MSA), with Large Language Models (LLMs) further accelerating progress in this field. Current approaches primarily leverage the knowledge and reasoning capabilities of parameter-heavy (Multimodal) LLMs for sentiment classification, overlooking autonomous multimodal sentiment reasoning generation in resource-constrained environments. Therefore, we focus on the Resource-Limited Joint Multimodal Sentiment Reasoning and Classification task, JMSRC, which simultaneously performs multimodal sentiment reasoning chain generation and sentiment classification only with a lightweight model. We propose a Multimodal Chain-of-Thought Reasoning Distillation model, MulCoT-RD, designed for JMSRC that employs a "Teacher-Assistant-Student" distillation paradigm to address deployment constraints in resource-limited environments. We first leverage a high-performance Multimodal Large Language Model (MLLM) to generate the initial reasoning dataset and train a medium-sized assistant model with a multi-task learning mechanism. A lightweight student model is jointly trained to perform efficient multimodal sentiment reasoning generation and classification. Extensive experiments on four datasets demonstrate that MulCoT-RD with only 3B parameters achieves strong performance on JMSRC, while exhibiting robust generalization and enhanced interpretability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the task of Resource-Limited Joint Multimodal Sentiment Reasoning and Classification (JMSRC). The authors note that existing approaches rely on large, resource-intensive Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs), which are impractical for deployment in resource-constrained environments. They propose MulCoT-RD, a framework that uses a "Teacher-Assistant-Student" distillation paradigm. A large MLLM (the teacher) generates reasoning chains, a medium-sized model (the assistant) is trained using multi-task learning on both sentiment classification and reasoning, and finally, a lightweight model (the student) is trained via joint learning to perform both tasks efficiently.  The paper presents experimental results on four datasets, demonstrating that the lightweight student model achieves competitive performance while maintaining interpretability and generalizability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty of the paper lies in its focus on resource-constrained JMSRC and the proposed "Teacher-Assistant-Student" distillation framework tailored for this problem. While reasoning distillation and knowledge distillation are established techniques, the application to multimodal sentiment analysis with a specific focus on *reducing model size for deployment* is a relevant contribution. The two-stage CoT enhancement module for improving the quality of the teacher's reasoning chains is also a valuable element.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from the increasing importance of deploying AI models in environments with limited computational resources. Achieving high performance with smaller models is crucial for real-world applications. The interpretability aspect is also important for understanding and trusting the model's predictions. While the individual components of the framework (knowledge distillation, multi-task learning) are not entirely new, their combination and adaptation to JMSRC address a practical and timely problem. The authors make a significant step towards enabling sentiment reasoning in resource-constrained environments.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-structured framework with detailed explanations of each component.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation across multiple datasets.
    *   Demonstration of improved interpretability through explicit reasoning chain generation.
    *   Evidence of good generalization ability.
    *   Emphasis on reducing model size for efficient deployment.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Limited exploration of different model architectures for the assistant and student models. The choice of models might be influenced by resource constraints, but investigating alternatives could further improve performance.
    *   The reliance on closed-source teacher models (e.g., GPT-4o) might limit reproducibility and accessibility for some researchers. While they also used an open-source alternative (Qwen), focusing solely on open-source models would enhance the paper's impact.
    *   The evaluation of reasoning quality could be more extensive. While metrics like Sim, METEOR, and ROUGE are used, a human evaluation of the generated reasoning chains would provide valuable insights.
    *   The specific trade-offs in performance versus model size are not fully explored.  A more detailed analysis of how the model size impacts various performance metrics would be valuable.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper could influence future research in several ways:

    *   By inspiring other researchers to focus on resource-constrained multimodal tasks.
    *   By providing a practical framework for reasoning distillation in MLLMs.
    *   By encouraging the development of more efficient and interpretable sentiment analysis models.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field by addressing the practical challenge of deploying multimodal sentiment analysis models in resource-constrained environments. The proposed framework is well-designed and supported by comprehensive experimental results. While the individual techniques are not entirely novel, their combination and adaptation to JMSRC, along with a focus on reducing model size and maintaining interpretability, constitute a meaningful advancement. The use of closed-source models and the possibility of a more detailed human evaluation slightly reduce the score.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### ArbiViewGen: Controllable Arbitrary Viewpoint Camera Data Generation for Autonomous Driving via Stable Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05236v1)
- **Authors**: Yatong Lan, Jingfeng Chen, Yiru Wang, Lei He
- **Abstract**: Arbitrary viewpoint image generation holds significant potential for autonomous driving, yet remains a challenging task due to the lack of ground-truth data for extrapolated views, which hampers the training of high-fidelity generative models. In this work, we propose Arbiviewgen, a novel diffusion-based framework for the generation of controllable camera images from arbitrary points of view. To address the absence of ground-truth data in unseen views, we introduce two key components: Feature-Aware Adaptive View Stitching (FAVS) and Cross-View Consistency Self-Supervised Learning (CVC-SSL). FAVS employs a hierarchical matching strategy that first establishes coarse geometric correspondences using camera poses, then performs fine-grained alignment through improved feature matching algorithms, and identifies high-confidence matching regions via clustering analysis. Building upon this, CVC-SSL adopts a self-supervised training paradigm where the model reconstructs the original camera views from the synthesized stitched images using a diffusion model, enforcing cross-view consistency without requiring supervision from extrapolated data. Our framework requires only multi-camera images and their associated poses for training, eliminating the need for additional sensors or depth maps. To our knowledge, Arbiviewgen is the first method capable of controllable arbitrary view camera image generation in multiple vehicle configurations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the ArbiViewGen paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "ArbiViewGen: Controllable Arbitrary Viewpoint Camera Data Generation for Autonomous Driving via Stable Diffusion Models" presents a novel framework for generating camera images from arbitrary viewpoints, addressing the challenge of limited ground-truth data in extrapolated views for autonomous driving. The method utilizes a diffusion-based approach and introduces two key components: Feature-Aware Adaptive View Stitching (FAVS) and Cross-View Consistency Self-Supervised Learning (CVC-SSL). FAVS combines geometric transformations with feature matching to create pseudo-ground truth data. CVC-SSL is a self-supervised learning paradigm that reconstructs original camera views from synthesized stitched images using a diffusion model, enforcing cross-view consistency without requiring ground-truth extrapolated data.  The authors propose a novel evaluation strategy based on projecting colored LiDAR point clouds to novel views. The approach aims to enable data reuse across different vehicle types, reducing development costs for new models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addressing a Crucial Problem:** The paper directly tackles a significant bottleneck in autonomous driving: the scarcity of ground-truth data for novel viewpoints, which hinders the training of high-fidelity generative models.
    *   **Novel Approach:** The proposed framework, ArbiViewGen, introduces a novel approach using diffusion models with a self-supervised learning scheme to generate controllable arbitrary viewpoint images without relying on ground-truth data at these viewpoints. This is a significant departure from existing methods that require such supervision.
    *   **Technical Innovation:** The FAVS and CVC-SSL components are technically sound and well-motivated. FAVS's hierarchical matching strategy and CVC-SSL's reconstruction-based learning seem promising. Using LiDAR data to validate synthesized views provides a quantitative analysis.
    *   **Practical Relevance:** The framework is designed to facilitate data reuse across different vehicle configurations, which has direct practical implications for reducing development costs and time in the autonomous driving industry.
    *   **Experimental Validation:** The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of ArbiViewGen on the nuScenes dataset, showcasing improvements over a baseline method and providing ablation studies to validate the contributions of each component.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Direct Comparison:** As mentioned in the paper, directly comparing ArbiViewGen with existing methods is difficult because its goals are more general. The baselines are limited in their function and do not provide a rigorous comparison with the SOTA performance. While DriveSuprim is relevant, it only scratches the surface on the vast field of 3D-aware view synthesis.
    *   **Reliance on Pose Accuracy:** The framework relies heavily on accurate camera pose information. Any errors in pose estimation can significantly impact the quality of view stitching and the effectiveness of self-supervised learning. The paper should discuss the sensitivity of the approach to pose errors and potential mitigation strategies. This is a critical and commonly encountered issue in real-world driving scenarios.
    *   **Simplifications and Approximations:** The use of far-field approximations and simplifications in the geometric transformation may introduce errors, particularly for objects closer to the camera.
    *   **Qualitative Evaluation:** While the quantitative results are promising, the qualitative results presented are somewhat limited. Additional qualitative comparisons, especially in challenging scenarios with occlusions, dynamic objects, and varying lighting conditions, would strengthen the evaluation.
    *   **Computational Cost:** Diffusion models are computationally intensive. The paper should discuss the computational cost of training and inference with ArbiViewGen, which is critical for practical deployment. The long training and inference of diffusion models make it difficult to validate quickly.
    *   **Limited Scale of Vehicle Setups:** The framework requires 6 cameras, however, the application can be broadened to other platforms or scale to more or less cameras.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The paper presents a **significant step forward** in the field of arbitrary viewpoint image generation for autonomous driving. The self-supervised learning approach for mitigating the need for ground-truth data at novel viewpoints is highly innovative.
    *   The combination of feature-aware stitching and cross-view consistency learning is a **novel and effective** way to improve the quality and consistency of generated images.
    *   The framework addresses a **practical and important challenge** in autonomous driving, enabling data reuse and reducing development costs.
    *   The proposed evaluation strategy based on projecting colored LiDAR points provides a **valuable tool** for evaluating the quality of generated images in extrapolated views.

**Overall Score:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of arbitrary viewpoint image generation for autonomous driving. The proposed framework addresses a crucial problem and demonstrates promising results on a challenging dataset. The self-supervised learning approach and the technical innovations of FAVS and CVC-SSL are particularly noteworthy. While the paper has some limitations in terms of the experimental evaluation and practical deployment issues, the strengths outweigh the weaknesses. Considering the novelty and the potential impact on the autonomous driving industry, a score of 7 is warranted.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Driver Assistant: Persuading Drivers to Adjust Secondary Tasks Using Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05238v1)
- **Authors**: Wei Xiang, Muchen Li, Jie Yan, Manling Zheng, Hanfei Zhu, Mengyun Jiang, Lingyun Sun
- **Abstract**: Level 3 automated driving systems allows drivers to engage in secondary tasks while diminishing their perception of risk. In the event of an emergency necessitating driver intervention, the system will alert the driver with a limited window for reaction and imposing a substantial cognitive burden. To address this challenge, this study employs a Large Language Model (LLM) to assist drivers in maintaining an appropriate attention on road conditions through a "humanized" persuasive advice. Our tool leverages the road conditions encountered by Level 3 systems as triggers, proactively steering driver behavior via both visual and auditory routes. Empirical study indicates that our tool is effective in sustaining driver attention with reduced cognitive load and coordinating secondary tasks with takeover behavior. Our work provides insights into the potential of using LLMs to support drivers during multi-task automated driving.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) as a "driving assistant" in Level 3 automated vehicles to proactively manage driver distraction and maintain appropriate alertness. The central idea is to use the LLM to deliver persuasive messages to the driver, based on real-time road conditions and detected driver behavior (eye-tracking data showing secondary task engagement). The system aims to provide "humanized" advice that is more effective than simple warning alerts by keeping the driver aware of potential risks and adjusting their secondary task engagement. The system was tested in a driving simulator with 36 participants, and the results suggest that the persuasive LLM reduces engagement in secondary tasks, stabilizes attention allocation (as measured by eye-tracking metrics), and is perceived positively by the drivers.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The application of LLMs for proactive driver behavior management, especially focusing on *persuasion* rather than just warnings, is relatively novel. While previous research has explored LLMs in autonomous driving, the specific focus on persuasive interaction based on road risk *and* driver distraction levels is a good contribution. However, similar approaches exist where LLMs are used for risk assessment and suggesting actions. The specific persuasive intervention is a good touch.

*   **Significance:** Addressing driver distraction in Level 3 automation is crucial, and this paper offers a potentially valuable approach. If persuasion proves more effective than traditional warnings, it could contribute to safer and more comfortable automated driving experiences. The use of LLMs for this purpose has the potential to lead to improved human-machine interaction in vehicles.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the challenge of driver distraction in L3 automation and the limitations of simple warning systems.
    *   **Integration of LLM and Sensor Data:** The system effectively combines real-time data (road conditions, eye-tracking) with LLM capabilities to generate context-aware persuasive messages.
    *   **Human-Centered Design:** The formative study (interviews) to understand driver preferences for persuasive strategies is a valuable component.
    *   **Empirical Evaluation:** The driving simulator experiment provides quantitative data (number of secondary tasks, eye-tracking metrics) to support the claims.
    *   **Positive Subjective Feedback:** The subjective questionnaire results indicate that drivers perceive the persuasive LLM positively.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Persuasion Strategies:** While the paper mentions several persuasive strategies (Status Feedback, Emphasize Risk, etc.), the actual implementation and variation of these strategies within the LLM-generated messages could be explored more deeply.
    *   **Virtual Environment:** The study was conducted in a simulated environment, which may not fully capture the complexities and nuances of real-world driving situations. The generalizability of the results to real-world scenarios needs further validation.
    *   **Short Experimental Duration:** The 20-minute experimental duration might not be sufficient to assess the long-term impact of the persuasive LLM on driver behavior and adaptation.
    *   **Sample Size:** While 36 is a decent sample size, a larger sample would increase statistical power.
    *   **Lack of comparison to other persuasive strategies:** The paper only compares against a warning system. It would be much stronger if the persuasive LLM could be compared with other persuasive systems based on other technologies or frameworks.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence the design of future driver assistance systems, particularly in the context of Level 3 and higher automation. The idea of using LLMs for personalized, proactive persuasion could be adopted by automotive manufacturers and technology developers. However, further research is needed to validate its effectiveness and address the limitations mentioned above.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper demonstrates a promising approach and provides initial evidence of its effectiveness, it is not without its limitations. The controlled virtual environment, short duration, and the lack of direct comparison with other persuasive systems slightly temper the enthusiasm. The novelty, while present, builds upon existing LLM research in driving and human-computer interaction.

Score: 6.5

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 6/10

### Pruning Large Language Models by Identifying and Preserving Functional Networks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05239v1)
- **Authors**: Yiheng Liu, Junhao Ning, Sichen Xia, Xiaohui Gao, Ning Qiang, Bao Ge, Junwei Han, Xintao Hu
- **Abstract**: Structured pruning is one of the representative techniques for compressing large language models (LLMs) to reduce GPU memory consumption and accelerate inference speed. It offers significant practical value in improving the efficiency of LLMs in real-world applications. Current structured pruning methods typically rely on assessment of the importance of the structure units and pruning the units with less importance. Most of them overlooks the interaction and collaboration among artificial neurons that are crucial for the functionalities of LLMs, leading to a disruption in the macro functional architecture of LLMs and consequently a pruning performance degradation. Inspired by the inherent similarities between artificial neural networks and functional neural networks in the human brain, we alleviate this challenge and propose to prune LLMs by identifying and preserving functional networks within LLMs in this study. To achieve this, we treat an LLM as a digital brain and decompose the LLM into functional networks, analogous to identifying functional brain networks in neuroimaging data. Afterwards, an LLM is pruned by preserving the key neurons within these functional networks. Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed method can successfully identify and locate functional networks and key neurons in LLMs, enabling efficient model pruning. Our code is available at https://github.com/WhatAboutMyStar/LLM_ACTIVATION.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a novel structured pruning method for large language models (LLMs) inspired by functional brain networks. The approach treats an LLM as a "digital brain" and decomposes it into functional networks using Independent Component Analysis (ICA), similar to how fMRI data is analyzed in neuroscience. The method then prunes the LLM by preserving the key neurons within these identified functional networks, aiming to minimize architectural disruption. Experimental results on various benchmark datasets and LLMs (Vicuna-7B-v1.5, LLaMA2-7B-chat-hf, ChatGLM3-6B-base) demonstrate that the proposed method achieves competitive performance compared to existing structured pruning techniques. The paper highlights the potential of applying neuroscience-inspired approaches to understand and improve LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the application of functional brain network analysis techniques (specifically ICA) to guide the structured pruning of LLMs.  While the analogy between neural networks and the brain is not entirely new, the specific translation of fMRI analysis methods, particularly the *CanICA* algorithm, to identify functional components in LLMs is relatively innovative. The paper successfully demonstrates the feasibility and potential benefits of this approach. However, it's crucial to acknowledge that the "novelty" relies on the adaptation of an existing technique from a different domain (neuroscience), so the fundamental novelty is somewhat incremental rather than groundbreaking.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the potential to improve the efficiency and interpretability of LLMs. Efficient pruning techniques are critical for deploying LLMs in resource-constrained environments. The neuroscience-inspired approach, if robust and generalizable, offers a potentially valuable alternative to existing importance-based or data-driven pruning methods.  Furthermore, by identifying "functional networks" within LLMs, the paper opens avenues for understanding the internal workings of these complex models, even if at a high level. However, the practical significance is limited by the need for calibration data. Performance improvements on standard benchmarks also aren't drastically better than SOTA pruning methods.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Interesting Analogy:** The "digital brain" analogy is conceptually appealing and provides a plausible rationale for the approach.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The paper clearly explains the steps involved in applying ICA to LLMs for identifying functional networks and guiding pruning.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experimental results provide evidence that the proposed method is competitive with state-of-the-art structured pruning techniques on several datasets and models.
    *   **Open Source:** Releasing the code enhances reproducibility and allows for further investigation by the research community.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Over-Reliance on Analogy:** While the brain analogy is helpful, it can be overstretched. The "functional networks" identified in LLMs may not have the same biological significance as those in the brain. The paper does not explore differences or potential pitfalls when using this analogy, which introduces a layer of risk in the application of the neuroscience based pruning method.
    *   **Limited Mechanistic Understanding:** The paper shows that the method works but offers limited insight into *why* it works. What are the characteristics of the identified functional networks? How do they contribute to the LLM's performance? More analysis in this direction could significantly strengthen the paper.
    *   **Computational Cost:**  Applying ICA, even layer-by-layer, still adds computational overhead. The paper does not thoroughly discuss the trade-offs between pruning performance and the computational cost of identifying the functional networks.
    *   **Benchmark improvements are incremental**.  The results do not offer a significant performance improvements over existing methods, instead the proposed method is competitive.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence the field by:
    *   Inspiring other researchers to explore neuroscience-inspired techniques for understanding and improving LLMs.
    *   Providing a new framework for interpreting the internal structure and function of LLMs.
    *   Spurring further research into efficient pruning methods that preserve the functional coherence of LLMs.

**Rigorous Rationale for the Score:**

While the paper presents a novel and promising approach, its impact is limited by the incremental performance improvements and the lack of deeper mechanistic understanding. While the neuroscience inspired pruning method offers a fresh angle of model compression, it also introduces a layer of potential pitfalls. Also the lack of a thorough computational analysis is problematic for a pruning method. The overall significance of the paper is therefore noteworthy, but not transformative.

Score: 6.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### CodeBoost: Boosting Code LLMs by Squeezing Knowledge from Code Snippets with RL
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05242v1)
- **Authors**: Sijie Wang, Quanjiang Guo, Kai Zhao, Yawei Zhang, Xin Li, Xiang Li, Siqi Li, Rui She, Shangshu Yu, Wee Peng Tay
- **Abstract**: Code large language models (LLMs) have become indispensable tools for building efficient and automated coding pipelines. Existing models are typically post-trained using reinforcement learning (RL) from general-purpose LLMs using "human instruction-final answer" pairs, where the instructions are usually from manual annotations. However, collecting high-quality coding instructions is both labor-intensive and difficult to scale. On the other hand, code snippets are abundantly available from various sources. This imbalance presents a major bottleneck in instruction-based post-training. We propose CodeBoost, a post-training framework that enhances code LLMs purely from code snippets, without relying on human-annotated instructions. CodeBoost introduces the following key components: (1) maximum-clique curation, which selects a representative and diverse training corpus from code; (2) bi-directional prediction, which enables the model to learn from both forward and backward prediction objectives; (3) error-aware prediction, which incorporates learning signals from both correct and incorrect outputs; (4) heterogeneous augmentation, which diversifies the training distribution to enrich code semantics; and (5) heterogeneous rewarding, which guides model learning through multiple reward types including format correctness and execution feedback from both successes and failures. Extensive experiments across several code LLMs and benchmarks verify that CodeBoost consistently improves performance, demonstrating its effectiveness as a scalable and effective training pipeline.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the CodeBoost paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CodeBoost, a novel post-training framework designed to enhance code large language models (LLMs) using only raw code snippets.  Unlike traditional post-training methods that rely on human-annotated instructions, CodeBoost leverages the abundance of readily available code snippets.  The framework incorporates several key components: (1) maximum-clique curation to create a diverse and representative training corpus, (2) bi-directional prediction learning from both forward (execution output prediction) and backward (code completion) tasks, (3) error-aware prediction enabling learning from both successful and failed executions, (4) heterogeneous augmentation to diversify code semantics, and (5) heterogeneous rewarding providing fine-grained supervision.  Experiments demonstrate that CodeBoost consistently improves the performance of existing code LLMs on various benchmarks, validating its effectiveness as a scalable and instruction-free training pipeline.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's main novelty lies in its attempt to build an instruction-free post-training framework for code LLMs by leveraging raw code snippets. This is a worthwhile direction given the scarcity of high-quality annotated coding instructions.  The individual components, such as bi-directional prediction, error-aware prediction, and heterogeneous augmentation, are not entirely novel in isolation. Bi-directional prediction has been explored in other contexts but its application in this setting is valuable. The combination of these techniques, specifically tailored to code LLMs and framed within a reinforcement learning setup, represents a meaningful contribution.

*   **Significance:** The potential significance of this work is that it offers a way to scale the post-training of code LLMs more efficiently, bypassing the bottleneck of human annotation.  A key strength is the rigorous empirical evaluation across various code LLMs and benchmarks. The ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of each component. A weakness is that the improvements achieved, while consistent, are often incremental.  The authors acknowledge some limitations (e.g., inability to handle visualization-centric code) which sets directions for future development. The reliance on GRPO (a specific RL technique) might limit generalisability.  It is also unclear if the improvements generalise to entirely unseen code or the performance improvement is primarily due to fine-tuning.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Addresses a critical problem: scarcity of instruction data for code LLMs.
    *   Offers a scalable, instruction-free alternative for post-training.
    *   Comprehensive experiments with diverse LLMs and benchmarks.
    *   Ablation studies provide insights into component effectiveness.
    *   The error-aware prediction aspect is a clever way to utilize information that's often discarded.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Individual components are not entirely novel; novelty lies in integration.
    *   The performance improvements, while consistent, are sometimes modest.
    *   Limited ability to handle visualization-centric code.
    *   Relies on a specific RL algorithm (GRPO), possibly limiting generalizability.
    *   Limited discussion of computational costs.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research by encouraging exploration of instruction-free methods for code LLM training. The insights into error-aware learning and the importance of diverse augmentation strategies are valuable contributions.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a valuable and well-executed study. While the individual components are not ground-breaking, the integration of these techniques into a comprehensive, instruction-free post-training framework for code LLMs is a significant contribution. The thorough empirical evaluation lends credibility to the findings. The consistent, if sometimes incremental, improvements across various models and benchmarks provide solid evidence for the effectiveness of the approach.

The limitations (e.g., visualisation-centric code) and the lack of complete theoretical novelty temper the score. Further work is needed to address these limitations and explore the generalizability of the approach to more complex coding scenarios and different RL algorithms. Also, a deeper dive on how the approach impacts code LLM reasoning would further strengthen the work. Thus, the score reflects the significance and thoroughness of the work balanced against the incremental nature of some of its components and some limitations.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Salt-Rock Creep Deformation Forecasting Using Deep Neural Networks and Analytical Models for Subsurface Energy Storage Applications
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05248v1)
- **Authors**: Pradeep Kumar Shukla, Tanujit Chakraborty, Mustafa Sari, Joel Sarout, Partha Pratim Mandal
- **Abstract**: This study provides an in-depth analysis of time series forecasting methods to predict the time-dependent deformation trend (also known as creep) of salt rock under varying confining pressure conditions. Creep deformation assessment is essential for designing and operating underground storage facilities for nuclear waste, hydrogen energy, or radioactive materials. Salt rocks, known for their mechanical properties like low porosity, low permeability, high ductility, and exceptional creep and self-healing capacities, were examined using multi-stage triaxial (MSTL) creep data. After resampling, axial strain datasets were recorded at 5--10 second intervals under confining pressure levels ranging from 5 to 35 MPa over 5.8--21 days. Initial analyses, including Seasonal-Trend Decomposition (STL) and Granger causality tests, revealed minimal seasonality and causality between axial strain and temperature data. Further statistical tests, such as the Augmented Dickey-Fuller (ADF) test, confirmed the stationarity of the data with p-values less than 0.05, and wavelet coherence plot (WCP) analysis indicated repeating trends. A suite of deep neural network (DNN) models (Neural Basis Expansion Analysis for Time Series (N-BEATS), Temporal Convolutional Networks (TCN), Recurrent Neural Networks (RNN), and Transformers (TF)) was utilized and compared against statistical baseline models. Predictive performance was evaluated using Root Mean Square Error (RMSE), Mean Absolute Error (MAE), Mean Absolute Percentage Error (MAPE), and Symmetric Mean Absolute Percentage Error (SMAPE). Results demonstrated that N-BEATS and TCN models outperformed others across various stress levels, respectively. DNN models, particularly N-BEATS and TCN, showed a 15--20\% improvement in accuracy over traditional analytical models, effectively capturing complex temporal dependencies and patterns.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary of the paper and critically evaluate its novelty and significance.

**Summary:**

This paper explores the application of deep learning (specifically, N-BEATS and TCN models) to predict the time-dependent deformation (creep) of salt rock under varying confining pressure conditions. Creep deformation is a critical factor in designing and operating underground storage facilities for various materials, including nuclear waste and hydrogen energy. The authors use multi-stage triaxial (MSTL) creep data from laboratory experiments, compare the performance of their deep learning models against traditional statistical and analytical models, and demonstrate a 15-20% improvement in accuracy with DNNs in capturing complex temporal dependencies. The paper also discusses the implications of their findings for the safe and efficient management of underground storage in salt rock formations. Statistical tests are conducted and models validated against each other using sophisticated metrics. The paper discusses limitations of both the analytical and DL models and suggests future work.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   *Application to a Relevant Problem:* Predicting salt rock creep is a practical problem with significant implications for energy storage and waste disposal.
    *   *Comparison of Methodologies:* The paper provides a comprehensive comparison of different forecasting methods, including deep learning, statistical, and analytical models.
    *   *Quantitative Results:* The paper presents quantitative results demonstrating the improved performance of deep learning models. Specifically comparing different flavors of DL models, NBEATS and TCN.
    *   *Rigorous Validation:* The use of MCB and the analysis framework appear relatively rigorous, giving confidence in the results.
    *   *Well-Written and Organized:* The paper is well-structured and clearly written, making it easy to follow the methodology and results.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   *Limited Novelty in Deep Learning Application:* While the application of deep learning to this specific problem is valuable, the paper doesn't introduce significant novelties in deep learning architectures or training techniques. They use existing established architectures like N-BEATS and TCN. The hyperparameters used are largely the defaults of the DL framework.
    *   *Data Limitations:* The study relies on laboratory data, which might not fully capture the complexities of in-situ conditions. Limited discussion on temperature variations that may skew long term results.
    *   *Limited Model Interpretability:* Although the paper mentions the interpretability of N-BEATS, it does not elaborate on how the model's internal representations contribute to better creep prediction. It provides limited discussions about why these models perform better than the analytical models.
    *   *Statistical Significance of the results:* Although the error plot of the MCB is discussed, there is limited discussion about the statistical significance of the results.
    *   *Comparison to SOTA models:* While different DL frameworks have been compared to each other, they don't test the models against SOTA literature data and results for this specific problem.

*   **Significance:**
    *   *Practical Implications:* The improved accuracy in creep prediction could lead to better designs and safer operation of underground storage facilities.
    *   *Demonstration of DL Applicability:* The paper provides evidence that deep learning can be a valuable tool for solving geoscience problems.
    *   *Benchmark for Future Research:* The comparison of different methodologies can serve as a benchmark for future research in this area. The paper may push other researchers to also try and replicate the experiments, adding value to the field.

**Overall Assessment and Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by demonstrating the applicability of deep learning to a relevant geoscience problem. The rigorous validation and comparison of different methodologies strengthen the findings. However, the limited novelty in deep learning techniques and the reliance on laboratory data limit the paper's overall impact. There is a lot of scope for future work by considering SOTA and novel methodologies. There is a general scope for improving all the DL and analytical models based on these results.

**Score: 6/10**

**Rationale:** The score reflects the paper's solid contribution to applying existing deep learning techniques to a relevant geoscience problem but acknowledges the limited novelty in the deep learning aspects and the need for more comprehensive data and analysis in future research. The value lies in the application and validation, but not in fundamentally advancing the field of deep learning or geomechanics.

- **Classification**: physics.geo-ph
- **Score**: 6/10

### MoBE: Mixture-of-Basis-Experts for Compressing MoE-based LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05257v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaodong Chen, Mingming Ha, Zhenzhong Lan, Jing Zhang, Jianguo Li
- **Abstract**: The Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architecture has become a predominant paradigm for scaling large language models (LLMs). Despite offering strong performance and computational efficiency, large MoE-based LLMs like DeepSeek-V3-0324 and Kimi-K2-Instruct present serious challenges due to substantial memory requirements in deployment. While recent works have explored MoE compression to address this issue, existing methods often suffer from considerable accuracy drops (e.g., 7-14% relatively) even at modest compression rates. This paper introduces a novel Mixture-of-Basis-Experts (MoBE) method that achieves model compression while incurring minimal accuracy drops. Specifically, each up/gate matrix in an expert is decomposed via a rank decomposition as W = AB, where matrix A is unique to each expert. The relatively larger matrix B is further re-parameterized as a linear combination of basis matrices {Bi} shared across all experts within a given MoE layer. The factorization is learned by minimizing the reconstruction error relative to the original weight matrices. Experiments demonstrate that MoBE achieves notably lower accuracy drops compared to prior works. For instance, MoBE can reduce the parameter counts of Qwen3-235B-A22B-2507, DeepSeek-V3-0324 (671B) and Kimi-K2-Instruct (1T) by 24%-30% with only 1%-2% accuracy drop (about 2% drops when measured relatively).
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MoBE (Mixture-of-Basis-Experts), a novel method for compressing MoE-based Large Language Models (LLMs). MoBE decomposes the up/gate matrices in each expert using rank decomposition (W = AB). It then re-parameterizes the larger matrix B as a linear combination of shared basis matrices across all experts within a MoE layer. This approach reduces parameters while aiming to preserve accuracy by minimizing reconstruction error. The authors demonstrate that MoBE achieves lower accuracy drops compared to existing compression techniques when applied to models like Qwen3-235B, DeepSeek-V3, and Kimi-K2-Instruct.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**  The paper presents a genuinely novel method for MoE compression. While rank decomposition and sharing parameters aren't new concepts *per se*, the specific application of *shared basis matrices* within the MoE architecture and the particular *two-stage decomposition approach* (rank decomposition followed by basis combination) appear to be a unique contribution. Compared to existing techniques like pruning, SVD-based methods, or more general knowledge distillation, MoBE introduces a different approach to balancing compression and performance. A significant strength lies in combining a expert-specific parameter matrix A with shared base matrix B. The method also innovates on the activation functions.

**Significance:**  The paper addresses a very significant problem: the large memory footprint of MoE-based LLMs, which hinders deployment, especially for larger models. Successfully compressing these models while minimizing performance loss is crucial for broader adoption and wider accessibility. The reported results, showing a substantial parameter reduction (24-30%) with only a small accuracy drop (1-2%), are impressive and indicate the potential for substantial practical impact. The paper provides thorough experimental results on a number of models of different sizes. The code is said to be released, which is commendable.

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Approach:** Introduces a new and potentially impactful compression method.
*   **Strong Results:**  Demonstrates compelling results on multiple leading MoE-based LLMs. The accuracy vs compression trade-off is improved compared to existing work.
*   **Detailed Analysis:**  Includes an analysis of the effective rank of weight matrices, justifying the need for their specific approach.
*   **Ablation Studies:** Ablation studies on activation functions demonstrate well-reasoned model building.
*   **Clear Presentation:** The paper clearly describes the MoBE method and experimental setup.
*   **Code Release:** Promises the release of training/inference code.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Computational Overhead:** The paper mentions that a fully optimized kernel isn't ready. Mimicking matrix factorization with existing kernels is relatively inefficient during inference. Without such optimization, its practical application might be limited initially.
*   **Limited Theoretical Foundation:** While the empirical results are strong, a deeper theoretical analysis explaining why the shared basis matrices work so well, and how to select the optimal number of basis matrices *m*, would strengthen the paper. While the analysis of the effective rank is helpful, it could be more closely tied to the choice of *m*.
*   **Choice of Rank r**: In the setup, the paper used *r=p*. While stated to be more simple, the justification of more compression if *r<p* needs better validation.
*   **Z-score Scaling Overhead:** It adds a layer of complexity, while is not considered for experimental comparison in prior works.
*   **Parameter Selection Heuristics**: The tuning of hyper-parameters (number of basis matrices, specific compression ratios) for each model seems heuristic.  A more systematic approach to parameter selection would make the method more robust and easier to use.

**Potential Influence:**  If the method is effective, generalizable, and efficient, it could significantly influence the development and deployment of large MoE-based LLMs, making them more accessible and easier to run on limited hardware. It may spawn further research on compression strategies tailored to MoE architectures.

**Score:** 7.5

**Rationale:** The paper presents a novel and potentially significant contribution to the field of MoE compression, with strong empirical results demonstrating improved accuracy vs. compression trade-offs. The primary weaknesses are the lack of theoretical grounding regarding the choice of parameters (especially *m*) and a full GPU kernel implementation. A strong focus on implementation efficiency and parameter selection strategies would be significant follow-up research, potentially justifying a higher score. The overall impact is very high because its performance in compressing large language models is excellent.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### SGDFuse: SAM-Guided Diffusion for High-Fidelity Infrared and Visible Image Fusion
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05264v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaoyang Zhang, Zhen Hua, Yakun Ju, Wei Zhou, Jun Liu, Alex C. Kot
- **Abstract**: Infrared and visible image fusion (IVIF) aims to combine the thermal radiation information from infrared images with the rich texture details from visible images to enhance perceptual capabilities for downstream visual tasks. However, existing methods often fail to preserve key targets due to a lack of deep semantic understanding of the scene, while the fusion process itself can also introduce artifacts and detail loss, severely compromising both image quality and task performance. To address these issues, this paper proposes SGDFuse, a conditional diffusion model guided by the Segment Anything Model (SAM), to achieve high-fidelity and semantically-aware image fusion. The core of our method is to utilize high-quality semantic masks generated by SAM as explicit priors to guide the optimization of the fusion process via a conditional diffusion model. Specifically, the framework operates in a two-stage process: it first performs a preliminary fusion of multi-modal features, and then utilizes the semantic masks from SAM jointly with the preliminary fused image as a condition to drive the diffusion model's coarse-to-fine denoising generation. This ensures the fusion process not only has explicit semantic directionality but also guarantees the high fidelity of the final result. Extensive experiments demonstrate that SGDFuse achieves state-of-the-art performance in both subjective and objective evaluations, as well as in its adaptability to downstream tasks, providing a powerful solution to the core challenges in image fusion. The code of SGDFuse is available at https://github.com/boshizhang123/SGDFuse.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "SGDFuse," a novel approach for infrared and visible image fusion (IVIF). It addresses the limitation of existing methods which often lack deep semantic understanding and introduce artifacts. SGDFuse leverages the Segment Anything Model (SAM) to generate high-quality semantic masks as explicit priors to guide a conditional diffusion model. This two-stage framework performs preliminary feature fusion, then utilizes SAM masks to guide coarse-to-fine denoising and generation, ensuring high-fidelity and semantically-aware image fusion. The authors demonstrate state-of-the-art performance in subjective, objective evaluations, and downstream tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in the *explicit* use of SAM-generated semantic masks as *direct* guidance within a conditional diffusion model for IVIF. While previous work has used diffusion models or semantic information *separately*, the *synergistic combination* with clear architecture design for high fidelity makes this approach unique. The two-stage framework and HFAH further contributes to novelty.
*   **Significance:** This is a significant contribution for several reasons:
    *   **Addresses Semantic Blindness:** It tackles a key limitation of many existing fusion methods that focus on pixel-level features and often fail to understand or preserve key targets semantically.
    *   **Improves Fidelity:** By using a diffusion model conditioned on semantic masks, the method effectively reconstructs high-fidelity, artifact-free images, which benefit both visual quality and downstream tasks.
    *   **Demonstrated Performance:** The paper provides strong empirical evidence of the method's superiority through extensive experiments on various datasets and tasks (object detection and semantic segmentation).
    *   **Downstream performance**: By evaluating the performance on downstream tasks the paper proves the practical application of this method and therefore it increases the importance of the work.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies and articulates the limitations of existing IVIF methods.
    *   **Well-Motivated Approach:**  The use of SAM and diffusion models is well-justified given their respective strengths. The approach logically connects semantic understanding with high-fidelity image generation.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** Thorough experiments on multiple datasets using diverse metrics validate the method's performance.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** Ablation studies effectively demonstrate the contribution of each component within the framework.
    *   **Code Availability:** Releasing the code contributes to reproducibility and facilitates further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Computational Cost:** Diffusion models are generally computationally expensive, which might limit real-time applications. The authors should explicitly address and quantify the computational cost of SGDFuse. A comparison to existing methods should be added.
    *   **Dependence on SAM:** The performance is directly tied to the performance of SAM. Limitations of SAM (e.g., failure cases, performance in specific scenarios) could affect the fusion results. This limitation could be overcome if the authors use a fine-tuned SAM to perform semantic segmentation, tailored for the task of image fusion.
    *   **Limited Ablation on Loss Function:** The second stage's loss function includes two weighting coefficients. A thorough analysis should be performed regarding the importance of these loss functions, and the value to assign to the coefficients.
    *   **Lack of comparison with other diffusion image fusion models:** Currently, the comparison is performed mostly agains methods that are not diffusion methods. It would be more valuable to add a comparison with other methods that use diffusion.
*   **Potential Impact:**
    *   **Advances IVIF:** This work could significantly advance the field of IVIF by establishing a more effective and semantically-aware paradigm.
    *   **Broader Applications:** The core idea of using semantic masks to guide diffusion-based image generation could be extended to other image fusion and image processing tasks.
    *   **Inspiration for Future Research:**  It provides a strong foundation for future research on IVIF, focusing on incorporating more advanced semantic understanding and efficient diffusion models.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the novelty of the synergistic SAM and diffusion model integration, the strong experimental results, the clear addressing of a known problem ("semantic blindness"), and potential for future impact, but acknowledging the computational cost and dependence on SAM, a score of 8/10 is justified. This is a significant contribution, demonstrating substantial improvements over existing methods and opening new avenues for research in image fusion.

**Score: 8**
